- NIST IR 8286A Rev. 1 Section 2.2.4 (Determining Potential Impact: leaders may want impact shown as the loss for each occurrence, the SLE, or as the yearly total, the ALE)
  - Verified, 5 Nov 2026, nvlpubs.nist.gov/nistpubs/ir/2025/NIST.IR.8286Ar1.pdf (Dec 2025), Section 2.2.4: "senior leaders may wish to see the impact of a risk expressed as the loss for each occurrence (the single loss expectancy, or SLE), or they might prefer to see the total loss for that risk over an annual period (the annualized loss expectancy, or ALE)"; the same passage notes that a frequent moderate event "may, over time, represent a higher risk than that of a major event that occurs infrequently", so frequency and per-event size are both shown. Used in crisc_d2_120 (a risk owner plans a yearly reserve equal to the ALE of a rare $1,000,000 ransomware loss; the analyst first shows the single loss).

### Round 13 additions (12 Nov 2026)
Items crisc_d2_122 to crisc_d2_126. Each new source was fetched on 12 Nov 2026 with `curl -sS -L` from nvlpubs.nist.gov and read in a text copy (`pdftotext -layout`); SP 800-150's status was checked on csrc.nist.gov (final, October 2016, not withdrawn). The batch also reuses existing bullets: NIST SP 800-30 Rev. 1 Section 2.3.1 (adversarial likelihood is based on intent, capability and targeting) and NIST CSF 2.0 ID.RA-02 (crisc_d2_122), NIST SP 800-30 Rev. 1 Section 3.1 and NIST IR 8286 Rev. 1 Section 3.5 Table 2 on transfer (crisc_d2_123), NIST SP 800-34 Rev. 1 Section 3.2.1 (re-read 12 Nov 2026: "the RTO must normally be shorter than the MTD"; "Unlike RTO, RPO is not considered as part of MTD") and ISACA Glossary (recovery point objective) (crisc_d2_124), ISACA Glossary (risk owner), ISACA Glossary (risk response) and NIST IR 8286 Rev. 1 Section 3 Table 1 (crisc_d2_125), and ISACA Glossary (risk response), ISACA Glossary (current risk) and NIST IR 8286 Rev. 1 Section 3.5.2 (crisc_d2_126).
- NIST SP 800-150 Section 2.1 (Threat Information Types: threat intelligence reports describe actors, TTPs and targeted systems, while indicators help detect and defend against threats)
  - Verified, 12 Nov 2026, nvlpubs.nist.gov/nistpubs/SpecialPublications/NIST.SP.800-150.pdf (Guide to Cyber Threat Information Sharing, October 2016), Section 2.1: indicators "are technical artifacts or observables that suggest an attack is imminent or is currently underway or that a compromise may have already occurred. Indicators can be used to detect and defend against potential threats", with IP addresses and file hashes as examples; security alerts are "technical notifications regarding current vulnerabilities, exploits, and other security issues"; "Threat intelligence reports are generally prose documents that describe TTPs, actors, types of systems and information being targeted", and threat intelligence provides "the necessary context for decision-making processes". Used in crisc_d2_122 (reports on groups targeting fund administrators, not indicators of compromise, inform risk scenarios).
- NIST SP 800-30 Rev. 1 Section 3.1 Task 1-2 (Identify Scope: scope is determined by the organizational official requesting the assessment and sets which decisions the results support)
  - Verified, 12 Nov 2026, nvlpubs.nist.gov/nistpubs/Legacy/SP/nistspecialpublication800-30r1.pdf (Sep 2012), Task 1-2 supplemental guidance: "Risk assessment scope affects the range of information available to make risk-based decisions and is determined by the organizational official requesting the assessment and the risk management strategy"; establishing scope determines "what decisions the assessment results support"; at Tier 2 the scope can cover "all systems, services, and infrastructures that support a specific mission/function". Used in crisc_d2_123 (a partner-hosted payment page outside the agreed scope goes back to the risk owner who set it).
- NIST SP 800-34 Rev. 1 Appendix B Section 3.1.1 (BIA Template, Identify Outage Impacts and Estimated Downtime: alternate means, such as a manual work-around, are described for each process)
  - Verified, 12 Nov 2026, nvlpubs.nist.gov/nistpubs/Legacy/SP/nistspecialpublication800-34r1.pdf (May 2010, updated Nov 2010), Appendix B (Sample BIA and BIA Template), Section 3.1.1: downtime is estimated "working directly with mission/business process owners", the RTO is the time a system resource can stay unavailable "before there is an unacceptable impact on other system resources, supported mission/business processes, and the MTD", and the template asks for "a description of any alternate means (secondary processing or manual work-around) for recovering the mission/business process(es) that rely on the system". Chapter 1 (Introduction) notes that performing business processes by alternate manual means is "typically acceptable for only short-term disruptions". Used in crisc_d2_124 (a four-hour RTO set without counting a two-day paper process).

### Round 15 additions (12 Nov 2026)
Items crisc_d2_133 to crisc_d2_137. The new source was fetched on 12 Nov 2026 with `curl -sS -L` from nvlpubs.nist.gov and read in a text copy (`pdftotext -layout`). The batch also reuses existing bullets: ISACA Glossary (recovery point objective) and NIST SP 800-34 Rev. 1 Section 3.2.1 (re-read 12 Nov 2026: RPO "is a factor of how much data loss the mission/business process can tolerate during the recovery process") in crisc_d2_133; NIST SP 800-30 Rev. 1 Appendix D Table D-2, ISACA Glossary (risk register) and NIST IR 8286 Rev. 1 Section 3.5 Table 2 on transfer in crisc_d2_134 (lightning damage kept out of the register because no one controls the weather); ISACA Glossary (residual risk) and NIST SP 800-53A Rev. 5 Section 2.4.2 (test as an assessment method) in crisc_d2_135 (a detective review that has found nothing); ISACA Glossary (loss event), NIST IR 8286 Rev. 1 Section 3.3.2 on secondary losses and COBIT 2019 APO12.01 in crisc_d2_136; NIST AI 100-2 E2025 Section 2.4.4 (Model Extraction) in crisc_d2_137, as a wrong option because the researchers receive the model itself.
- NIST AI 100-2 E2025 Section 2.4.2 (Membership Inference: learning that an individual's record was in the training set can itself expose private information, as in a medical study)
  - Verified, 12 Nov 2026, nvlpubs.nist.gov/nistpubs/ai/NIST.AI.100-2e2025.pdf (Adversarial Machine Learning: A Taxonomy and Terminology of Attacks and Mitigations, March 2025), Section 2.4.2: membership inference attacks "may expose private information about an individual" and "are of great concern when releasing aggregate information or ML models trained on user data"; "determining that an individual is part of the training set already has privacy implications, such as in a medical study of patients with a rare disease"; the attacker's goal "is to determine whether a particular record or data sample was part of the training dataset"; attacks can run in white-box settings, "in which attackers have knowledge of the model's architecture and parameters", as well as through queries. Used in crisc_d2_137 (a generic drug maker plans to give researchers a copy of its model trained on patients' adverse-reaction reports).

### Round 16 additions (12 Nov 2026)
Items crisc_d2_138 to crisc_d2_143. Each new source was fetched on 12 Nov 2026 with `curl -sS -L` from nvlpubs.nist.gov and read in a text copy (`pdftotext -layout`). The batch also reuses existing bullets: ISACA Glossary (vulnerability) (crisc_d2_138); ISACA Glossary (risk scenario) and NIST IR 8286A Rev. 1 Section 2.2.5 (crisc_d2_139); NIST SP 800-30 Rev. 1 Section 2.3.1 on non-adversarial likelihood and on predisposing conditions (crisc_d2_140, re-read 12 Nov 2026: "For other than adversarial threat events, the likelihood of occurrence is estimated using historical evidence, empirical data, or other factors"); ISACA Glossary (business impact analysis) (crisc_d2_141); NIST IR 8286 Rev. 1 Section 3.8 (crisc_d2_142, re-read 12 Nov 2026: "applying a consistent risk register with agreed-upon criteria and categories enables various data points to be normalized, aggregated, and sorted into an enterprise view"); NIST SP 800-30 Rev. 1 Section 3.1 Task 1-5 from the Domain 1 block (crisc_d2_143).
- NIST AI 100-1 (AI RMF 1.0) Section 3.1 (Valid and Reliable: measures of accuracy should consider false positive and false negative rates, and results may be disaggregated by data segment)
  - Verified, 12 Nov 2026, nvlpubs.nist.gov/nistpubs/ai/NIST.AI.100-1.pdf (AI RMF 1.0, January 2023), Section 3.1, page 14: "Measures of accuracy should consider computational-centric measures (e.g., false positive and false negative rates), human-AI teaming, and demonstrate external validity (generalizable beyond the training conditions)"; "Accuracy measurements should always be paired with clearly defined and realistic test sets – that are representative of conditions of expected use"; "Accuracy measurements may include disaggregation of results for different data segments." Used in crisc_d2_138 (a steel recycler's AI camera that flags lithium batteries in under 1% of scrap loads: the miss count on known batteries, not overall accuracy, shows how well it works).
- NIST SP 800-34 Rev. 1 Section 3.2 (Conduct the Business Impact Analysis: the BIA should be performed during the Initiation phase of the SDLC and may be repeated as the design evolves)
  - Verified, 12 Nov 2026, nvlpubs.nist.gov/nistpubs/Legacy/SP/nistspecialpublication800-34r1.pdf (May 2010, updated Nov 2010), Section 3.2: "The BIA should be performed during the Initiation phase of the SDLC. As the system design evolves and components change, the BIA may need to be conducted again during the Development/Acquisition phase of the SDLC"; the same section says the BIA results are used "to determine contingency planning requirements and priorities". Used in crisc_d2_141 (a crop insurer's new policy portal: the BIA comes before the hosting and recovery design is settled).
- NIST SP 800-34 Rev. 1 Appendix F (Contingency Planning and the SDLC: considering the recovery strategy during initial design reduces costs and avoids retrofitting)
  - Verified, 12 Nov 2026, same PDF, Appendix F, Development/Acquisition Phase: "By considering the recovery strategy during the initial design, costs are reduced and problems associated with retrofitting or modifying the system during the Operation/Maintenance phase are minimized"; under Initiation, "the mission/business processes that the new information system will support should be evaluated to determine the users' recovery time requirements." Used in crisc_d2_141.
- NIST IR 8286 Rev. 1 Section 3 Table 1 (Risk Category: an organizing construct that lets multiple register entries be consolidated by common theme or topic)
  - Verified, 12 Nov 2026, nvlpubs.nist.gov/nistpubs/ir/2025/NIST.IR.8286r1.pdf (December 2025), Section 3, Table 1 (Descriptions of notional cybersecurity risk register template elements), Risk Category: "An organizing construct that enables multiple risk register entries to be consolidated by common theme or topic"; "Consistent risk categorization is helpful for comparing risk registers during the risk aggregation step of ERM." Used in crisc_d2_142 (an e-scooter operator's board wants its supplier-related risks shown together).
- NIST SP 800-30 Rev. 1 Section 2.3.2 (Assessment Approaches: unless each qualitative value is clearly defined or characterized by meaningful examples, different experts could produce significantly different results)
  - Verified, 12 Nov 2026, nvlpubs.nist.gov/nistpubs/Legacy/SP/nistspecialpublication800-30r1.pdf (Sep 2012), Section 2.3.2 on qualitative assessments: "unless each value is very clearly defined or is characterized by meaningful examples, different experts relying on their individual experiences could produce significantly different assessment results. The repeatability and reproducibility of qualitative assessments are increased by the annotation of assessed values". Used in crisc_d2_143 (a veterinary practice group's impact scale names five levels only, and its clinic managers' ratings of one outage span all five).

### Round 17 additions (12 Nov 2026)
Items crisc_d2_144 to crisc_d2_148. Each new source was fetched on 12 Nov 2026 with `curl -sS -L` from nvlpubs.nist.gov and read in a text copy (`pdftotext -layout`); the csrc.nist.gov page for SP 800-84 was opened the same day and lists it as final (September 2006), not withdrawn. The batch also reuses existing bullets: ISACA Glossary (residual risk) (crisc_d2_144); NIST AI 100-2 E2025 Section 2.3 on targeted and backdoor poisoning (crisc_d2_145); NIST SP 800-34 Rev. 1 Section 3.5.3 on tabletop exercises (crisc_d2_146); NIST SP 800-34 Rev. 1 Section 3.6 on updating the BIA after a significant change (crisc_d2_147, re-read 12 Nov 2026: "When a significant change occurs, the BIA should be updated with the new information to identify new contingency requirements or priorities"); NIST SP 800-30 Rev. 1 Section 3.1 Task 1-4 on internal and external sources (crisc_d2_148).
- NIST SP 800-30 Rev. 1 Section 3.4 Task 4-2 (Update Risk Assessment: updates assess how selected risk factors have changed, such as newly affected assets, likelihoods and impacts)
  - Verified, 12 Nov 2026, nvlpubs.nist.gov/nistpubs/Legacy/SP/nistspecialpublication800-30r1.pdf (Sep 2012), Section 3.4, Task 4-2 supplemental guidance (page 39): "Organizations determine the frequency and the circumstances under which risk assessments are updated"; updates identify and assess "only how selected risk factors have changed, for example: (i) the identification of new threat events, vulnerabilities, predisposing conditions, undesirable consequences and/or affected assets; and (ii) the assessments of threat source characteristics (e.g., capability, intent, targeting, range of effects), likelihoods, and impacts." Used in crisc_d2_144 (a contract research lab's sample database, rated for coded animal data, now also holds named patients' trial records under the same tested controls).
- NIST AI 100-2 E2025 Section 3.2.2 (Model Poisoning Attacks: maliciously designed pre-trained models can carry backdoors that persist after downstream fine-tuning)
  - Verified, 12 Nov 2026, nvlpubs.nist.gov/nistpubs/ai/NIST.AI.100-2e2025.pdf (March 2025), Section 3.2.2 (page 42): "it is common for developers to use foundation models developed by third parties. Attackers can take advantage of this fact by offering maliciously designed models, such as pre-trained models that enable a BACKDOOR POISONING ATTACK or TARGETED POISONING ATTACK"; "researchers have identified attacks in which malicious backdoors in pre-trained models can persist even after downstream users fine-tune the model for their own use". Section 2.3.4 (Model Poisoning, "Supply chain model poisoning") says models or components provided by suppliers can be poisoned, and that the GenAI supply-chain discussion applies to predictive models too. Used in crisc_d2_145 (a geological survey tunes a hub-downloaded open-weight model on its own slope photos).
- NIST AI 100-2 E2025 Section 3.2.3 (Mitigations: risks can be reduced by understanding models as untrusted system components)
  - Verified, 12 Nov 2026, same PDF, Section 3.2.3 (pages 42-43): traditional supply chain practices "such as vulnerability scanning of model artifacts can help manage some kinds of AI supply chain risks, new approaches are required to detect vulnerabilities in models such as those introduced through model poisoning attacks"; "risks can be reduced by understanding models as untrusted system components and designing applications such that risks from attacker-controlled model outputs are reduced". Section 4.2.2 adds that organizations "may not be able to audit and identify vulnerabilities encoded into a model's weights in the same way it is often possible to audit open-source software". Used in crisc_d2_145 (a hub's download count is not evidence that a model is clean).
- NIST SP 800-84 Section 4.3 (Develop the Tabletop Exercise Material: the scenario is a narrative of a hypothetical incident meant to inspire responses that demonstrate the exercise objectives)
  - Verified, 12 Nov 2026, nvlpubs.nist.gov/nistpubs/Legacy/SP/nistspecialpublication800-84.pdf (Guide to Test, Training, and Exercise Programs for IT Plans and Capabilities, September 2006), Section 4.3 (page 4-3), facilitator guide contents: "The exercise's scenario, which is a sequential, narrative account of a hypothetical incident that provides the catalyst for the exercise and is intended to introduce situations that will inspire responses and thus allow demonstration of the exercise objectives". Section 4.2.3 sets tabletop objectives as validating the plan's content, participants' roles and responsibilities, and interdependencies. Used in crisc_d2_146 (an irrigation district's first tabletop exercise, meant to test managers' escalation and decisions).
- NIST SP 800-84 Section 5.3 (Develop the Functional Exercise Material: scenarios may explore worst cases, but scenarios on topical issues participants are apt to meet in the real world are often useful)
  - Verified, 12 Nov 2026, same PDF, Section 5.3 (pages 5-3 to 5-4), Scenario: "The scenario is designed to add realism to the exercise"; "Exercise scenarios may be crafted to explore worst-case situations; however, it is often useful to develop scenarios that cause participants to respond to topical issues they are apt to encounter in the real world", for example a hurricane-caused power outage for an organization prone to natural disasters. Stated for functional exercises. Cited by crisc_d2_146 (an implausible dam collapse against a top-ranked peak-season pump outage) until the round 17 standards audit (12 Nov 2026), which dropped it there because the item is a tabletop exercise; kept as opened text, citable for a functional-exercise item.
- NIST SP 800-34 Rev. 1 Section 3.6 (Plan Maintenance: the plan is reviewed for accuracy and completeness at an organization-defined frequency or whenever significant changes occur)
  - Verified, 12 Nov 2026, nvlpubs.nist.gov/nistpubs/Legacy/SP/nistspecialpublication800-34r1.pdf (May 2010, updated Nov 2010), Section 3.6 (page 31): "As a general rule, the plan should be reviewed for accuracy and completeness at an organization-defined frequency or whenever significant changes occur to any element of the plan"; the plan is "reviewed and updated regularly as part of the organization's change management process". Used in crisc_d2_147 (a patent office's like-for-like scanner swap meets none of its BIA's revision triggers, so it waits for the scheduled update).
- NIST IR 8286A Rev. 1 Section 2.3.2.1 (Improving Estimation Based on Knowledge of Prior Events: practitioners can review a system's actual records of past events to inform likelihood estimates)
  - Verified, 12 Nov 2026, nvlpubs.nist.gov/nistpubs/ir/2025/NIST.IR.8286Ar1.pdf (December 2025), Section 2.3.2.1 (pages 50-51): "Information about previous risk events may be helpful when estimating the likelihood and impact of those in the future"; in the worked example, "Risk practitioners can review the actual availability of that website for the previous year (using a table similar to Table 9)", and "While past performance is not a guarantee of future probability, it provides some information that helps inform likelihood estimates." Used in crisc_d2_148 (a recruitment firm's CRM records of every CV sent, matched to consents, show how often CVs go out without consent).

### Round 18 additions (12 Nov 2026)
Items crisc_d2_149 to crisc_d2_154. Each new source was fetched on 12 Nov 2026 with `curl -sS -L` from nvlpubs.nist.gov and read in a text copy (`pdftotext -layout`). The batch also reuses existing bullets: NIST IR 8286A Rev. 1 Section 2.2.3 (Vulnerability Identification) (crisc_d2_149); NIST IR 8286A Rev. 1 Section 2.2.5 (Recording Identified Risks: risk scenarios in cause-and-effect form) (crisc_d2_150); NIST SP 800-30 Rev. 1 Section 3.4 (Maintaining the Risk Assessment) (crisc_d2_151); NIST IR 8286A Rev. 1 Section 2.3.1 (Selecting Risk Analysis Methodologies) and NIST SP 800-30 Rev. 1 Section 2.3.2 (Assessment Approaches: quantitative assessments support cost-benefit analysis of risk responses) (crisc_d2_153); ISACA Glossary (residual risk) (crisc_d2_154).
- NIST SP 800-30 Rev. 1 Section 2.3.1 (Risk Models: the severity of a vulnerability depends on the adverse impact if it is exploited, so it is context-dependent)
  - Verified, 12 Nov 2026, nvlpubs.nist.gov/nistpubs/Legacy/SP/nistspecialpublication800-30r1.pdf (Sep 2012), Section 2.3.1, Vulnerability and Predisposing Condition, footnote 25 (page 9): "The severity of a vulnerability is an assessment of the relative importance of mitigating/remediating the vulnerability. The severity can be determined by the extent of the potential adverse impact if such a vulnerability is exploited by a threat source. Thus, the severity of vulnerabilities, in general, is context-dependent." The same page says vulnerabilities "are not identified only within information systems". Used in crisc_d2_149 (a bottled-gas distributor's phishing tests: finance clerks who release payments click less often than drivers whose handhelds open only route lists).
- NIST AI 100-1 (AI RMF 1.0) Section 3.7 (Fair – with Harmful Bias Managed: bias can occur without discriminatory intent and can amplify harms to individuals, groups, organizations and society)
  - Verified, 12 Nov 2026, nvlpubs.nist.gov/nistpubs/ai/NIST.AI.100-1.pdf (January 2023), Section 3.7 (pages 17-18): "Fairness in AI includes concerns for equality and equity by addressing issues such as harmful bias and discrimination"; NIST's three categories of AI bias (systemic, computational and statistical, human-cognitive) "can occur in the absence of prejudice, partiality, or discriminatory intent"; "AI systems can potentially increase the speed and scale of biases and perpetuate and amplify harms to individuals, groups, communities, organizations, and society." Used in crisc_d2_150 (a tax-preparation firm's refund-advance model declines self-employed filers twice as often as similar salaried filers; the scenario names the model, the declines, the group and the consequence).
- NIST SP 800-30 Rev. 1 Section 3.4 Task 4-2 (Update Risk Assessment: unless significant changes call for revisiting the whole assessment, updates assess only how selected risk factors have changed)
  - Verified, 12 Nov 2026, same SP 800-30 Rev. 1 PDF, Section 3.4, Task 4-2 supplemental guidance (page 39): "If significant changes (as defined by organizational policies, direction, or guidance) have occurred since the risk assessment was conducted, organizations can revisit the purpose, scope, assumptions, and constraints of the assessment to determine whether all tasks in the risk assessment process need to be repeated. Otherwise, the updates constitute subsequent risk assessments, identifying and assessing only how selected risk factors have changed", such as new threat events and affected assets. Task 4-1 (page 38) says monitoring results "can be used to refresh risk assessments at whatever frequency deemed appropriate". Used in crisc_d2_151 (a weather service redoes its full radar network assessment yearly though monitoring shows only a new threat group and two retired radars).
- NIST IR 8286 Rev. 1 Section 3.8 (Creating and Maintaining an Enterprise-Level Cybersecurity Risk Register: risk guidance declares the frequency with which the register will be updated)
  - Verified, 12 Nov 2026, nvlpubs.nist.gov/nistpubs/ir/2025/NIST.IR.8286r1.pdf (December 2025), Section 3.8 (page 41): enterprise risk guidance "should declare the role responsible for creating and maintaining the enterprise risk register, the frequency with which the register will be updated, and how the risks within the register will be communicated to various stakeholders." Used in crisc_d2_152 (a notary office's entries are reviewed every six months or sooner on a stated trigger; none has fired for the e-signing fraud entry IT wants re-rated now).
- NIST IR 8286 Rev. 1 Section 3.6.1 (Continuous Risk Monitoring: monitoring that shows a risk approaching its tolerance level triggers a review of the risk)
  - Verified, 12 Nov 2026, same IR 8286 Rev. 1 PDF, Section 3.6.1 (page 34): "enterprise risk conditions should be continually monitored to ensure that they remain within acceptable levels. For example, such monitoring could determine when negative cybersecurity risks for a system are approaching the risk tolerance level, triggering a review of the risk that could result in a higher priority for the risk and the implementation of additional risk responses." Used in crisc_d2_152 (an early review follows a trigger such as monitoring results; a request on its own is not one).
- NIST IR 8286 Rev. 1 Section 3.5.1 (Applying Security Controls to Reduce Risk Exposure: corrective controls offset the impact of consequences after a risk event, while deterrent controls reduce the likelihood of a threat event)
  - Verified, 12 Nov 2026, same IR 8286 Rev. 1 PDF, Section 3.5.1 (page 31), examples of controls: "Deterrent: Reduce the likelihood of a threat event by dissuading a threat actor"; "Detective: Provide warning of a successful or attempted threat event"; "Corrective: Reduce exposure by offsetting the impact of consequences after a risk event". Used in crisc_d2_154 (a pest-control firm's residual ransomware rating cuts likelihood as well as impact for restore-tested offline backups, which act on impact).

### Round 19 additions (12 Nov 2026)
Items crisc_d2_155 to crisc_d2_160. The NIST sources were fetched on 12 Nov 2026 with `curl -sS -L` from nvlpubs.nist.gov and read in text copies (`pdftotext -layout`); the FIRST CVSS v4.0 User Guide was fetched the same day from first.org (https://www.first.org/cvss/v4.0/user-guide; version history to v1.2) and read in the HTML. The batch also reuses existing bullets: NIST IR 8286A Rev. 1 Section 2.2.4 on secondary impacts such as regulators' fines and ISACA Glossary (loss event) (crisc_d2_156); NIST SP 800-30 Rev. 1 Section 3.2 Task 2-2 (crisc_d2_157); NIST SP 800-30 Rev. 1 Section 2.3.1 on context-dependent vulnerability severity (crisc_d2_158); ISACA Glossary (maximum tolerable outage) (crisc_d2_159); ISACA Glossary (residual risk) (crisc_d2_160).
- NIST AI 100-1 (AI RMF 1.0) MAP 1.1 (intended purposes, context-specific laws, norms and expectations, and the settings where the AI system will be deployed are understood and documented)
  - Verified, 12 Nov 2026, nvlpubs.nist.gov/nistpubs/ai/NIST.AI.100-1.pdf (AI RMF 1.0, January 2023), Table 2 (MAP function), page 26: category MAP 1 "Context is established and understood"; MAP 1.1 "Intended purposes, potentially beneficial uses, context-specific laws, norms and expectations, and prospective settings in which the AI system will be deployed are understood and documented", with considerations including "assumptions and related limitations about AI system purposes, uses, and risks". Used in crisc_d2_155 (a wastewater plant rates a vendor's AI model in its own dosing use before relying on the vendor's low rating for advisory use).
- NIST AI 100-1 (AI RMF 1.0) Section 1.2.1 (Risk Measurement: a deployer's particular use of a pre-trained model may entail risks that differ from those the developer perceived)
  - Verified, 12 Nov 2026, same AI RMF 1.0 PDF, Section 1.2.1 (Risk Measurement), page 6: "an AI developer who makes AI software available, such as pre-trained models, can have a different risk perspective than an AI actor who is responsible for deploying that pre-trained model in a specific use case. Such deployers may not recognize that their particular uses could entail risks which differ from those perceived by the initial developer." The same section adds that measurements in a laboratory or controlled environment "may differ from risks that emerge in operational, real-world settings". Used in crisc_d2_155.
- NIST IR 8286A Rev. 1 Section 2.2.2.1 (Threat Enumeration: logical and physical threat sources are considered, as are threat conditions that supply chain partners such as external service providers might introduce)
  - Verified, 12 Nov 2026, nvlpubs.nist.gov/nistpubs/ir/2025/NIST.IR.8286Ar1.pdf (December 2025), Section 2.2.2.1, page 29: "both logical and physical threat sources should be considered"; among the example considerations, "Are there threat conditions that might be introduced by supply chain partners, such as external service providers?" Used in crisc_d2_157 (an exam board's threat model stops at its repository, though a contractor prints the papers and couriers deliver them).
- FIRST Common Vulnerability Scoring System (CVSS) v4.0 User Guide, Vulnerability Chaining (CVSS rates individual vulnerabilities, and for a chain the analyst may combine scores using the least-restrictive Exploitability and most-impactful Impact metrics)
  - Verified, 12 Nov 2026, https://www.first.org/cvss/v4.0/user-guide opened and read, section "Vulnerability Chaining": "CVSS is designed to classify and rate individual vulnerabilities"; for "multiple vulnerabilities ... exploited in the course of a single attack", "it is acceptable to combine two or more CVSS scores to describe the chain of vulnerabilities by assessing the least-restrictive Exploitability metrics and assessing the most-impactful Impact metrics." Used in crisc_d2_158 (three CVSS-medium flaws chained to reach clients' files are rated as one route).
- NIST SP 800-34 Rev. 1 Section 3.2.1 (Determine Business Processes and Recovery Criticality: the time to reprocess data after an outage is added to the RTO to stay within the MTD)
  - Verified, 12 Nov 2026, nvlpubs.nist.gov/nistpubs/Legacy/SP/nistspecialpublication800-34r1.pdf (May 2010, updated Nov 2010), Section 3.2.1, page 17: "Because the RTO must ensure that the MTD is not exceeded, the RTO must normally be shorter than the MTD. For example, a system outage may prevent a particular process from being completed, and because it takes time to reprocess the data, that additional processing time must be added to the RTO to stay within the time limit established by the MTD." NIST's MTD is the ISACA Glossary's MTO. Used in crisc_d2_159 (a flour mill's draft RTO equals the order MTO, leaving no time to clear held orders).
- NIST IR 8286A Rev. 1 Section 2.3.2 (Techniques for Estimating Likelihood and Impact: a range of possible values is more helpful and likely more accurate than a single point estimate)
  - Verified, 12 Nov 2026, same IR 8286A Rev. 1 PDF, Section 2.3.2, page 50: "Some industry specialists have indicated that a range of possible values is more helpful and likely more accurate than a single 'point estimate'"; the same passage recommends calibration that helps "reduce overconfidence". Used in crisc_d2_160 (a model showing $0 residual loss because a tested gateway was entered as 100% effective).

- NIST SP 800-40 Rev. 4 Section 3.3 (Define Risk Response Scenarios: routine patching covers patches not elevated to emergency status, while emergency patching is for crises such as a severe or actively exploited vulnerability)
  - Verified, 12 Nov 2026, nvlpubs.nist.gov/nistpubs/SpecialPublications/NIST.SP.800-40r4.pdf (Guide to Enterprise Patch Management Planning: Preventive Maintenance for Technology, April 2022), Section 3.3, printed pages 11 to 12: routine patching "is the standard procedure for patches that are on a regular release cycle and have not been elevated to emergency status. Most patching falls under this scenario"; routine patching is "often postponed and neglected", which "provides many additional windows of opportunity for attackers"; emergency patching is "the procedure to address patching emergencies in a crisis situation, such as a severe vulnerability or a vulnerability being actively exploited". Section 3.6 adds that a new vulnerability's relative importance decides whether it is "addressed by routine patching versus emergency patching or mitigations". Used in crisc_d2_167 (a juice maker's medium-severity pasteurizer-control flaw, with no network path confirmed this week and no known exploitation, goes in the next scheduled cycle).
- NIST IR 8286 Rev. 1 Section 3 (Cybersecurity Risk Considerations Throughout the ERM Process: a risk detail record documents considerations, assumptions and results so the risk register stays a summary)
  - Verified, 12 Nov 2026, nvlpubs.nist.gov/nistpubs/ir/2025/NIST.IR.8286r1.pdf (December 2025), Section 3, text after Table 1, printed pages 13 to 14: "A risk detail record documents the considerations, assumptions, and results of risk management activities to keep the formal risk register a summary rather than a large, detailed report"; "there should be a connection between the data in the risk register and the risk detail report", whose contents may include "a detailed risk scenario description and underlying threats, vulnerabilities, assets threatened". Used in crisc_d2_169 (a cryptocurrency exchange's entries run to pages of attack paths, assumptions and notes, so owners skip their reviews; the detail moves to linked records).

## Domain 3 Risk Response and Reporting (pilot crisc_d3_001 to 020, 30 Oct 2026)


### ISACA Interactive Glossary (https://www.isaca.org/resources/glossary)
- ISACA Glossary (risk transfer)
- ISACA Glossary (risk owner)
- ISACA Glossary (risk response)
- ISACA Glossary (risk aggregation)
- ISACA Glossary (compensating control)
- ISACA Glossary (detective control)
- ISACA Glossary (key risk indicator)
- ISACA Glossary (control owner)
  - All Verified, 30 Oct 2026: the glossary page on isaca.org was opened and each entry read. Risk transfer: assigning risk to another enterprise, usually by insurance or outsourcing, also known as risk sharing (001). Risk owner: the person given authority and accountability for risk-based decisions who owns the loss of a realized scenario (005). Risk response: any combination of avoidance, acceptance, sharing or transfer, or mitigation so that as much future residual risk as possible, usually depending on budgets, falls within appetite (010). Risk aggregation: integrating risk assessments at a corporate level for a complete view of overall enterprise risk (014). Compensating control: an internal control that reduces the risk of an existing or potential control weakness (007). Detective control: controls designed to detect and report when errors, omissions and unauthorized uses or entries occur (008). Key risk indicator (KRI): a subset of risk indicators that are highly relevant and have a high probability of predicting or indicating important risk (015). Control owner (added 31 Oct 2026, entry read on the same glossary page): the person given authority and accountability for control-related decisions, responsible for ensuring the control is implemented and operating effectively and efficiently (crisc_d3_005, re-angled to control ownership). Risk owner is now cited by crisc_d3_005 alongside it.

### NIST SP 800-39, Managing Information Security Risk (Mar 2011, no revision)
- NIST SP 800-39 Section 3.3 (Responding to Risk: accept, avoid, mitigate, share or transfer)
  - Verified, 30 Oct 2026, against nistspecialpublication800-39.pdf (nvlpubs.nist.gov), Section 3.3 Task 3-1: responses are acceptance, avoidance, mitigation, sharing, transfer or a combination; risk transfer "reduces neither the likelihood of harmful events occurring nor the consequences"; avoidance eliminates the activities or technologies that are the basis for the risk. Same string as the CC library. Used in crisc_d3_001. crisc_d3_003 moved to NIST IR 8286B-upd1 Sections 2.3.2 and 2.3.4 on 30 Oct 2026 (audit), because Section 3.3 read alone says avoidance may be appropriate when risk exceeds tolerance, which backs that item's runner-up.
- NIST SP 800-39 Section 3.3 Task 3-2 (Evaluation of Alternatives: effectiveness, feasibility and cost)
  - Verified, 30 Oct 2026, same PDF: Task 3-2 evaluates each course of action on expected effectiveness and feasibility, including mission or business impact and costs over the period the course of action is followed, as a risk versus risk-response trade-off. Used in crisc_d3_009 (root-cause control chosen for effectiveness) and crisc_d3_010 (control that meets appetite at lower cost).
- NIST SP 800-39 Section 3.4 (Monitoring Risk: compliance, response effectiveness and risk-impacting changes)
  - Verified, 30 Oct 2026, same PDF: risk monitoring verifies compliance, determines the ongoing effectiveness of risk responses and identifies risk-impacting changes; Tier 1 and 2 monitoring includes changes in the threat space and analyses of new technologies. Used in crisc_d3_020 (new AI rules under consultation as a risk-impacting change).

### NIST IR 8286B-upd1, Prioritizing Cybersecurity Risk for Enterprise Risk Management (Feb 2025)
- NIST IR 8286B-upd1 Section 2.3.1 (Risk Acceptance: exposure within risk tolerance is still reported, monitored and managed)
- NIST IR 8286B-upd1 Section 2.3.2 (Risk Avoidance: not conducting the activity when exposure exceeds tolerance)
- NIST IR 8286B-upd1 Section 2.3.4 (Risk Mitigation: preventive, deterrent, detective, corrective and compensating controls)
- NIST IR 8286B-upd1 Section 2.4.3 (Risk Owner: risk escalation and elevation)
  - All Verified, 30 Oct 2026, against NIST.IR.8286B-upd1.pdf (nvlpubs.nist.gov; csrc.nist.gov lists Feb 2025, superseding the Feb 2022 original). 2.3.1: acceptance is first considered when exposure is already acceptable under risk tolerance, and the risk "must be reported, monitored, and managed" (crisc_d3_002). 2.3.2: if exposure exceeds tolerance, the risk owner may decide not to conduct the activity; the text notes it is rare that no mix of transfer and mitigation would work (crisc_d3_003). 2.3.4: mitigation applies controls listed as preventative, deterrent, detective, corrective and compensating, with a definition and example for each (crisc_d3_003, crisc_d3_008, crisc_d3_009). 2.4.3: the risk owner column names who ensures the response is implemented; escalation happens when a threshold or time frame is reached, and elevation moves the decision to a more senior stakeholder (cited by crisc_d3_005 and crisc_d3_016 until 31 Oct 2026; both items were re-angled in the cross-domain fix). Used in crisc_d3_024, crisc_d3_029 and crisc_d1_042.

### NIST IR 8286C Rev. 1, Staging Cybersecurity Risks for Enterprise Risk Management and Governance Oversight (Dec 2025; supersedes IR 8286C-upd1, withdrawn 18 Dec 2025)
- NIST IR 8286C Rev. 1 Section 2.2 (Normalization of Cybersecurity Risk Registers: the same rating criteria enable comparison and tracking)
  - Verified, 31 Oct 2026, against NIST.IR.8286Cr1.pdf (nvlpubs.nist.gov/nistpubs/ir/2025/NIST.IR.8286Cr1.pdf; the CSRC page https://csrc.nist.gov/pubs/ir/8286/c/r1/final lists it final on 18 Dec 2025, superseding IR 8286C-upd1 of Sep 2022, updated 6 Mar 2024). Section 2.2, now titled "Normalization of Cybersecurity Risk Registers", says normalization at the higher level "should use the same rating criteria (ordinal, categorical, etc.) to enable comparison and tracking", and its value-mapping example maps a high, medium and low scale onto 1, 2 and 3; Section 2.1 requires a source ID so each aggregated risk can be traced back to its original register. Used in crisc_d3_014. Replaces the withdrawn IR 8286C-upd1 Section 2.2 bullet (concept review D3-1, quality pass 31 Oct 2026).

- NIST SP 800-53 Rev. 5 RA-7 (Risk Response)
  - Verified, 30 Oct 2026, against NIST.SP.800-53r5.pdf (nvlpubs.nist.gov): respond to findings in accordance with organizational risk tolerance; options include mitigating, accepting with justification, sharing or transferring, or avoiding. Same string as the CC library. Used in crisc_d3_002.
- NIST SP 800-53 Rev. 5 CA-2 (Control Assessments)

- NIST SP 800-53 Rev. 5 CA-5 (Plan of Action and Milestones)

  - Verified, 30 Oct 2026, same PDF: the control includes response actions to address the results of analysis of monitoring information and reporting status to defined roles. Same string as the CC library. Used in crisc_d3_018.
- NIST SP 800-53 Rev. 5 CA-7(4) (Continuous Monitoring | Risk Monitoring)
  - Verified, 30 Oct 2026, same PDF: risk monitoring is part of the continuous monitoring strategy (effectiveness, compliance and change monitoring) and "is informed by the established organizational risk tolerance". ### NIST SP 800-137, Information Security Continuous Monitoring (Sep 2011)

- NIST SP 800-137 Section 3.5 (Respond to Findings)

### Round 2 additions (crisc_d3_021 to 050, 31 Oct 2026)
- ISACA Glossary (risk acceptance)
- ISACA Glossary (preventive control)
- ISACA Glossary (key control indicator)
  - All Verified, 31 Oct 2026: https://www.isaca.org/resources/glossary downloaded and each entry read. Risk acceptance: a decision to accept a risk, made according to the risk appetite and risk tolerance set by senior management (crisc_d3_021, crisc_d3_024). Preventive control: an internal control used to avoid undesirable events, errors and other occurrences (crisc_d3_032). Key control indicator: a measure of the effectiveness of controls that indicates a failure or weakness which may increase the likelihood or impact of risk events (crisc_d3_042, crisc_d3_045).
- NIST CSF 2.0 Section 3.1 (Organizational Profiles step 4: analyze the gaps between the Current and Target Profiles and develop a prioritized action plan)
- NIST CSF 2.0 Section 3.2 (CSF Tiers: progression to higher Tiers is encouraged when risks or mandates are greater or when cost-benefit analysis supports it)
  - Both Verified, 31 Oct 2026, against https://nvlpubs.nist.gov/nistpubs/CSWP/NIST.CSWP.29.pdf (re-downloaded). Section 3.1 step 4: "Conduct a gap analysis to identify and analyze the differences between the Current and Target Profiles, and develop a prioritized action plan" (crisc_d3_034). Section 3.2: Tiers characterize the rigor of risk governance and management practices; "Progression to higher Tiers is encouraged when risks or mandates are greater or when a cost-benefit analysis indicates a feasible and cost-effective reduction of negative cybersecurity risks" (crisc_d3_035, by analogy: Tiers are organization-level rigor, applied here to risk-based targets). The existing D4 bullet for Section 3.1 (Current and Target Profiles) is reused by crisc_d3_033.
- NIST SP 800-137 Section 3.2.2 (Establish Monitoring and Assessment Frequencies: control volatility, impact level, known weaknesses, risk tolerance and threat information)
  - Verified, 31 Oct 2026, against https://nvlpubs.nist.gov/nistpubs/Legacy/SP/nistspecialpublication800-137.pdf (re-downloaded): organizations set each control's assessment frequency and each metric's monitoring frequency from security control volatility, system impact level, critical functions, identified weaknesses, organizational risk tolerance, threat and vulnerability information (crisc_d3_046).
- NIST SP 800-55v1 Section 3.1.4 (Data Quality: a validation process checks the integrity, accuracy and structure of data before any analysis is done)
  - Verified, 31 Oct 2026, against https://nvlpubs.nist.gov/nistpubs/SpecialPublications/NIST.SP.800-55v1.pdf (re-downloaded): data validity is suspect when the source holds information from only a few organizational elements; "Having a validation process in place to check the integrity, accuracy, and structure of the data provides a way to address potential errors before any analysis is done" (crisc_d3_040, crisc_d3_041).
- NIST IR 8286B-upd1 Section 2.2.4 (Considerations of Positive Risks as an Input to ERM)
- NIST IR 8286B-upd1 Section 2.2.5 (Visualizing Risk Priority: heat maps are a quick-reference guide with limited ability to convey risk information)
  - Both Verified, 31 Oct 2026, against https://nvlpubs.nist.gov/nistpubs/ir/2025/NIST.IR.8286B-upd1.pdf (re-downloaded). 2.2.4: risks of all types, positive and negative, are included in communications and prioritization so senior managers see uncertainties that might bring benefit or harm (crisc_d3_050). 2.2.5: heat maps "should be used with caution"; colors and positions are "a guide for quick reference", and a matrix based on likelihood and impact "is equally limited" (crisc_d3_047).
- NIST IR 8286C Rev. 1 Section 5.1 (Key CSRM Mechanisms: risk tolerance is translated into security controls, KPIs and KRIs)
- NIST IR 8286C Rev. 1 Section 5.2 (Monitoring Risks: KRIs are observed for trends such as rising likelihood or failing controls)
  - Both Verified, 31 Oct 2026, against https://nvlpubs.nist.gov/nistpubs/ir/2025/NIST.IR.8286Cr1.pdf (re-downloaded). 5.1: tolerance statements are translated into the triad of security controls, KPIs (measures that show program success) and KRIs (measures valuable for predicting risk) (crisc_d3_043). 5.2: "KRIs can be observed to monitor trends", and a practitioner who sees a KRI change checks whether likelihood or severity is rising, a new risk has entered, or controls are failing (crisc_d3_045, crisc_d3_047).
- NIST SP 800-30 Rev. 1 Section 2.3.1 (Risk Models: uncertainty can be communicated by giving ranges of values rather than single values)
- NIST SP 800-30 Rev. 1 Section 3.2 Task 2-6 (Determine Risk: several moderate-level risks can aggregate to a higher level of risk)
  - Both Verified, 31 Oct 2026, against https://nvlpubs.nist.gov/nistpubs/Legacy/SP/nistspecialpublication800-30r1.pdf (re-downloaded). 2.3.1, Uncertainty: the degree of uncertainty "can be communicated ... by providing ranges of values rather than single values for identified risks" (crisc_d3_049). Task 2-6: "when multiple risks materialize, even if each risk is at the moderate level, the set of those moderate-level risks could aggregate to a higher level of risk" (crisc_d3_030).

### Round 3 additions (crisc_d3_051 to 085, 4 Nov 2026)
- ISACA Glossary (control risk self-assessment)
- ISACA Glossary (risk map)
- ISACA Glossary (Capability Maturity Model)
- ISACA Glossary (maturity)
  - All Verified, 4 Nov 2026: https://www.isaca.org/resources/glossary fetched with `curl -sS -L` and each entry read. Control risk self-assessment: a method by which management and staff collectively identify and evaluate risk and controls within their business areas, possibly under the guidance of a facilitator such as a risk manager (crisc_d3_077: managers rate their own controls, the risk function facilitates). Risk map: a graphic tool for ranking and displaying risk by defined ranges for frequency and magnitude (crisc_d3_079: a ranking tool, so its scores are not averaged). Capability Maturity Model (CMM): describes an evolutionary improvement path from ad hoc, immature processes to disciplined, mature processes (crisc_d3_067). Maturity: the degree of reliability a business can place on a process to achieve its goals (crisc_d3_067).
- NIST CSF 2.0 GV.SC-10 (cybersecurity supply chain risk management plans include provisions for activities that occur after the conclusion of a partnership or service agreement)
  - Verified, 4 Nov 2026, against https://nvlpubs.nist.gov/nistpubs/CSWP/NIST.CSWP.29.pdf (re-downloaded), GV.SC-10 subcategory text read. Used in crisc_d3_055 (vendor access removal as a required step when a contract ends).
- NIST AI 100-1 (AI RMF 1.0) GOVERN 6.2 (contingency processes are in place to handle failures or incidents in third-party data or AI systems deemed to be high-risk)
- NIST AI 100-1 (AI RMF 1.0) Appendix C (AI Risk Management and Human-AI Interaction: some AI systems may not require human oversight while others specifically require it)
  - Both Verified, 4 Nov 2026, against https://nvlpubs.nist.gov/nistpubs/ai/NIST.AI.100-1.pdf (re-downloaded). GOVERN 6.2 (Table 1, under GOVERN 6 on third-party software, data and supply chain risks): "Contingency processes are in place to handle failures or incidents in third-party data or AI systems deemed to be high-risk" (crisc_d3_056). Appendix C, item 1: human-AI configurations span fully autonomous to fully manual; "Some AI systems may not require human oversight ... Other systems may specifically require human oversight" (crisc_d3_062, review scaled to the impact of a wrong output, cited with GOVERN 3.2).
- NIST SP 800-53B Section 2.4 (Tailoring Control Baselines: align the baseline with organizational risk and record a risk-based justification for each decision)
  - Verified, 4 Nov 2026, against https://nvlpubs.nist.gov/nistpubs/SpecialPublications/NIST.SP.800-53B.pdf (Sep 2020, updates as of 10 Dec 2020; still the current final on csrc.nist.gov). Section 2.4: after selecting a baseline, organizations tailor it to align controls with their requirements, as part of risk management; tailoring includes scoping, compensating controls, parameter values and "Supplementing baselines with additional controls and control enhancements"; controls are not removed arbitrarily, and tailoring decisions with their risk-based justification are documented. The "Supplementing Control Baselines" subsection says additional controls may be required to address specific threats to organizations and mission and business processes. Used in crisc_d3_060 (tailor rather than apply the high baseline unchanged everywhere, cited with Section 2.2) and crisc_d3_061 (add controls for a scenario the baseline misses).
- NIST SP 800-53B Section 2.2 (Selecting Control Baselines: each system's baseline is selected from its impact level, low, moderate or high)
  - Verified, 4 Nov 2026, against the same PDF (re-downloaded). Section 2.2: "Once the impact level of the system is determined, organizations select the appropriate security control baseline"; the organization selects one of three baselines "corresponding to the low-impact, moderate-impact, or high-impact categorization of the system". Used in crisc_d3_060 (each system's baseline selected by impact, then tailored).
- NIST SP 800-137 Section 3.1 (Define ISCM Strategy: the strategy is developed in line with organizational risk tolerance and reviewed on trigger criteria as well as a preestablished frequency)
  - Verified, 4 Nov 2026, against https://nvlpubs.nist.gov/nistpubs/Legacy/SP/nistspecialpublication800-137.pdf (re-downloaded; summary corrected on review the same day). Section 3.1: as changes occur, the ISCM strategy is reviewed for relevance and accuracy in reflecting organizational risk tolerances, and it includes "criteria describing the conditions that trigger a review or update of the strategy, in addition to the preestablished frequency audit"; 3.1.1: the strategy "is developed and implemented to support risk management in accordance with organizational risk tolerance". These triggers are for reviewing the strategy itself, not for reassessing risk. Used in crisc_d3_078 (a risk-based monitoring strategy before switching on 600 checks).
- NIST SP 800-137 Section 3.2.2 (Establish Monitoring and Assessment Frequencies: event-driven assessments on incidents, new threat information and significant changes, alongside the set frequencies)
  - Verified, 4 Nov 2026, against the same PDF (re-downloaded). The "Event-Driven Assessments" subsection of 3.2.2: events "may occur that trigger the immediate need to assess security controls or verify security status outside of requirements expressed in the ISCM strategy"; criteria for event-driven assessments consider "incidents, new threat information, significant changes to systems and operating environments, new or additional mission responsibilities, and results of a security impact analysis (SIA) or assessment of risk"; the rest of 3.2.2 sets the planned frequencies. Used in crisc_d3_076 (scheduled reviews kept alongside event triggers). The round 2 bullet for 3.2.2 (frequency factors) stays for crisc_d3_046 and crisc_d3_078.
- NIST SP 800-55v1 Section 3.1.1 (Measures Documentation: a standard record of what is measured, the data source, the formula and who handles the data keeps measures traceable)
- NIST SP 800-55v1 Section 3.1.2 (Measurement Reporting: context such as the risk indicated, fit with risk appetite and the decision needed)
  - Both Verified, 4 Nov 2026, against https://nvlpubs.nist.gov/nistpubs/SpecialPublications/NIST.SP.800-55v1.pdf (Dec 2024, re-downloaded). 3.1.1: measures are documented in a standard format; "By keeping a consistent record of what is being measured, where the data comes from, what formulas and calculations are being used, and who interacts with the data, it becomes easier to trace data"; fields include formula, data source and responsible parties (crisc_d3_071 one definition for a KRI across units; crisc_d3_072 system data with recorded lineage). 3.1.2: a measure reported without context can cause indecision, so a report can include the risk indicated, whether findings fit the risk appetite and "What prioritization, action, or decision might need to be taken" (crisc_d3_080, crisc_d3_082).
  - Note: other round 3 parts reuse bullets already in this library: ISACA Glossary (control owner), (risk owner), (policy), (risk aggregation) and (key risk indicator); NIST CSF 2.0 GV.RR-02, GV.SC-05, GV.PO-01, GV.OC-01, ID.RA-02, ID.RA-06, ID.RA-07 and Section 3.1 (step 4); NIST SP 800-53 Rev. 5 CA-2, CA-5, RA-7 and SA-9; NIST SP 800-53A Rev. 5 Section 2.4.2; NIST SP 800-39 Sections 3.3 Task 3-2 and 3.4; NIST SP 800-30 Rev. 1 Sections 2.3.1, 2.3.2, 3.3 and 3.4; NIST SP 800-137 Sections 3.2.2 and 3.4; NIST SP 800-55v1 Sections 3.1.4 and 4.4; NIST IR 8286 Rev. 1 Section 3.5.2; NIST IR 8286B-upd1 Section 2.2.5; NIST IR 8286C Rev. 1 Sections 2.2 and 5.2; NIST AI 100-1 GOVERN 2.1, GOVERN 3.2 and MANAGE 3.1.

### Round 6 additions (crisc_d3_116 to 128, 4 Nov 2026)
- NIST IR 8286B-upd1 Section 2.3.3 (Risk Transfer: considered when a risk cannot be accepted or fully avoided, most often through insurance)
- NIST IR 8286B-upd1 Section 2.4.1 (Risk Response Cost: the cost of a response is compared with the risk exposure to support a cost-benefit analysis)
  - Both Verified, 4 Nov 2026, against https://nvlpubs.nist.gov/nistpubs/ir/2025/NIST.IR.8286B-upd1.pdf (Feb 2025, downloaded with `curl -sS -L` and read as text). 2.3.3: "If a risk in the register cannot be accepted or fully avoided, another option would be to determine if some or all of the exposure could be transferred to (or shared with) another entity. The most frequent example of this activity is the use of an insurance provision"; the Table 4 notes add that insurance reimburses some losses while reputational consequences remain. 2.4.1: the risk response cost column "enables comparison with the risk exposure rating value and supports a cost-benefit analysis"; "An estimation of the cost of response against the likely loss exposure had the response not occurred helps support risk decisions". Used in crisc_d3_116 (cover costing more than it saves on a risk whose worst plausible loss is within appetite, cited with 2.3.1).
- NIST SP 800-53 Rev. 5 Section 1.1 (Purpose and Applicability: a comprehensive and flexible catalog of security and privacy controls)
- NIST CSF 2.0 Section 1 (CSF Overview: the Core is a taxonomy of high-level outcomes and does not prescribe how they are achieved)

SP 800-53 Rev. 5 (https://nvlpubs.nist.gov/nistpubs/SpecialPublications/NIST.SP.800-53r5.pdf, re-downloaded) Section 1.1, "Purpose and Applicability": the publication helps organizations identify the controls needed to manage risk "by providing a comprehensive and flexible catalog of security and privacy controls"; Section 1.6 says Chapter Three "provides a consolidated catalog of security and privacy controls". CSF 2.0 (https://nvlpubs.nist.gov/nistpubs/CSWP/NIST.CSWP.29.pdf, re-downloaded) Section 1, "Cybersecurity Framework (CSF) Overview": the CSF Core "is a taxonomy of high-level cybersecurity outcomes", and the document "does not prescribe outcomes nor how they may be achieved"; the abstract says "The CSF does not prescribe how outcomes should be achieved". Used in crisc_d3_137 (a patch KRI rising during a holiday change freeze, compared with the same months of past years before it is called a trend), cited with IR 8286C Rev. 1 Section 5.2.
- NIST SP 800-53B Section 2.4 (Assigning Control Parameter Values: parameter values may be driven by mission or business requirements)
  - Verified, 5 Nov 2026, against https://nvlpubs.nist.gov/nistpubs/SpecialPublications/NIST.SP.800-53B.pdf (Sep 2020, updated 10 Dec 2020; downloaded and read as text). Section 2.4, "Tailoring Control Baselines", lists "Assigning values to organization-defined control parameters via explicit assignment and selection operations" among tailoring activities; its "Assigning Control Parameter Values" part says organizations "determine the appropriate organization-defined values" and that "The parameter values may be driven by mission or business requirements, or the values may be prescribed by laws, executive orders, directives, regulations, policies, standards, guidelines, or industry best practices." "Concentration risk: The concern that an enterprise becomes reliant on a small number of suppliers to provide technology or other services, creating dependency on the suppliers." Used in crisc_d3_144 (defining concentration risk for a new procurement head).

- NIST IR 8286C Rev. 1 Section 2.3 (Analysis of Cybersecurity Risk Registers: aggregated summarization should not impede managers' review of specific risk decisions)
  - Verified, 5 Nov 2026, against https://nvlpubs.nist.gov/nistpubs/ir/2025/NIST.IR.8286Cr1.pdf (Dec 2025; downloaded with `curl -sS -L` and read as text). Section 2.3, "Analysis of Cybersecurity Risk Registers": "Aggregated summarization is a valuable reporting tool, but it should not impede the ability of managers to review specific risk decisions." The same section says a combined representative risk must describe what is at risk, "since the loss of internal business documents, patient healthcare records, and employee financial information might each have different likelihoods and impacts", and that aggregation formulas "are not intended to take the place of management experience and prudent judgement". Used in crisc_d3_158 (twelve regional outage risks, one critical, rolled into one enterprise line), cited with ISACA Glossary (risk aggregation). Fit note: NIST allows combining similar risks (for example by three-point estimation); the item faults hiding one risk whose impact differs, not combining as such.
  - Fit checks, 5 Nov 2026, for reused bullets. NIST SP 800-53 Rev. 5 (https://nvlpubs.nist.gov/nistpubs/SpecialPublications/NIST.SP.800-53r5.pdf, re-downloaded) SR-6 discussion: "The reviews may be conducted by the organization or by an independent third party." Used in crisc_d3_154 (relying on an independent, full-year SOC 2 Type 2 report that covers the service and its host, with the user controls run). NIST SP 800-55v1 (https://nvlpubs.nist.gov/nistpubs/SpecialPublications/NIST.SP.800-55v1.pdf, re-downloaded) Section 3.1.2 and Appendix C.3 were re-read for crisc_d3_160 (a KRI shown as a 12-month rolling average that hid two red months; per-period values show the level and the noise that the average smooths).
  - Note: the other round 10 parts reuse bullets already in this library: ISACA Glossary (control owner), (detective control), (risk aggregation) and (key risk indicator); NIST CSF 2.0 GV.RR-02, GV.SC-07 and ID.RA-06; NIST SP 800-53 Rev. 5 CA-5 and SR-6; NIST IR 8286B-upd1 Section 2.3.4; NIST SP 800-55v1 Section 3.1.2 and Appendix C.3; NIST AI 100-1 MEASURE 2.11 (Domain 4 block).

### Round 17 additions (12 Nov 2026)

- NIST AI 100-1 (AI RMF 1.0) Section 3.5 (Explainable and Interpretable: risks to interpretability can be addressed by communicating why the system made a particular prediction or recommendation)
  - Verified, 12 Nov 2026, against https://nvlpubs.nist.gov/nistpubs/ai/NIST.AI.100-1.pdf (Jan 2023), Section 3.5, printed pages 16 to 17: explainability is "a representation of the mechanisms underlying AI systems' operation" and interpretability "the meaning of AI systems' output in the context of their designed functional purposes"; "Risks to interpretability often can be addressed by communicating a description of why an AI system made a particular prediction or recommendation"; transparency answers "what happened", explainability "how" and interpretability "why" a decision was made. ## Domain 4 Technology and Security (pilot crisc_d4_001 to 020, 30 Oct 2026)


Bullets in the library format. Paste under the CRISC library's `---` line. Every
bullet below was opened on 30 Oct 2026: the NIST PDF was downloaded from
nvlpubs.nist.gov with `curl -sS -L`, converted with pdftotext and the control,
subcategory or section read. Paraphrases inside the parentheses are our own short
summaries of the official text. Bullets also present in the CC library
(`data/cc/originals/_framework_library.md`) are marked "re-listed".

### ISACA Glossary (https://www.isaca.org/resources/glossary), added 31 Oct 2026
- ISACA Glossary (shadow IT)
- ISACA Glossary (problem)
- ISACA Glossary (user acceptance testing)
- ISACA Glossary (data owner)
- ISACA Glossary (recovery time objective)
- ISACA Glossary (segregation/separation of duties)
- ISACA Glossary (purpose limitation)
- ISACA Glossary (current risk)
  - All Verified, 31 Oct 2026: the glossary page was fetched from isaca.org with `curl -sS -L` and each entry read. Shadow IT: systems, services, hardware or software used on the enterprise network or infrastructure "without proper vetting and approval from the IT or cybersecurity department" (crisc_d4_001). Problem: "In IT, the unknown underlying cause of one or more incidents" (crisc_d4_004). User acceptance testing: functional testing where software "is tested by the intended audience or business representative" (crisc_d4_005). Data owner: "Individual(s) responsible for the integrity, accuracy, and use of data within an organization" (crisc_d4_007; crisc_d4_008). Recovery time objective: "The amount of time allowed for the recovery of a business function or resource after a disaster occurs" (crisc_d4_011; notes 4A6.1). Segregation/separation of duties: a basic internal control that assigns initiating and recording transactions and custody of assets to separate individuals (crisc_d4_015). Purpose limitation: data collected for specified, explicit and legitimate purposes and not further processed in an incompatible way (crisc_d4_019). Current risk: the risk state now, counting actions already taken but not those anticipated or proposed (crisc_d4_016; re-listed from the D2 block). The same page also defines maximum tolerable outage (MTO), acceptable interruption window (AIW), need-to-know and the shared responsibility model, used in the D4 notes.

### NIST SP 800-53 Rev. 5, Security and Privacy Controls (Sep 2020; catalog release 5.2.0, Aug 2025)
- NIST SP 800-53 Rev. 5 AC-5 (Separation of Duties)
  - Verified, 30 Oct 2026 (re-listed from CC): control text read; separation of duties "addresses the potential for abuse of authorized privileges" by dividing functions among individuals. Used in crisc_d4_005 (developers running acceptance tests) and crisc_d4_015 (one person runs the payment cycle).
- NIST SP 800-53 Rev. 5 CM-3 (Configuration Change Control)
  - Verified, 30 Oct 2026 (re-listed from CC): review and approve or disapprove configuration-controlled changes with explicit consideration of security and privacy impact analyses; retain records; monitor change activity. Used in crisc_d4_003.
- NIST SP 800-53 Rev. 5 CM-4 (Impact Analyses)
  - Verified, 30 Oct 2026: "Analyze changes to the system to determine potential security and privacy impacts prior to change implementation"; the discussion includes risk assessments to decide whether more controls are needed. Used in crisc_d4_006 (a vendor's AI model swap treated as a change).
- NIST SP 800-53 Rev. 5 CP-8(2) (Telecommunications Services | Single Points of Failure)
  - Verified, 30 Oct 2026: "Obtain alternate telecommunications services to reduce the likelihood of sharing a single point of failure with primary telecommunications services." Used in crisc_d4_011 (recovery site on the same carrier).
- NIST SP 800-53 Rev. 5 MP-6 (Media Sanitization)
  - Verified, 30 Oct 2026: control title and text read (sanitize media before disposal, release or reuse). No item cites it since the 31 Oct 2026 re-audit; kept for later batches.
- NIST SP 800-53 Rev. 5 PT-3 (Personally Identifiable Information Processing Purposes)
  - Verified, 30 Oct 2026 (re-listed from CC): identify and document the purposes for processing PII and restrict processing to what is compatible with them. Used in crisc_d4_019 (health inferences for ads).
- NIST SP 800-53 Rev. 5 RA-8 (Privacy Impact Assessments)
  - Verified, 30 Oct 2026: conduct PIAs before developing or procuring IT that processes PII; the discussion calls a PIA "a living document that organizations update whenever changes to the information technology, changes to the organization's practices, or other factors alter the privacy risks". Used in crisc_d4_020.
- NIST SP 800-53 Rev. 5 SA-3 (System Development Life Cycle)
  - Verified, 30 Oct 2026: use an SDLC that incorporates security and privacy, define roles and responsibilities throughout it, and integrate the risk management process into SDLC activities. Used in crisc_d4_005.
- NIST SP 800-53 Rev. 5 SA-22 (Unsupported System Components)
  - Verified, 30 Oct 2026 (re-listed from CC): replace components when support ends or provide alternative support; the discussion notes missing patches give adversaries an opportunity. Used in crisc_d4_002.
- NIST SP 800-53 Rev. 5 SI-12 (Information Management and Retention)
  - Verified, 31 Oct 2026 (re-listed from CC; text first read 30 Oct 2026 for the D4 notes and re-read in the SP 800-53 Rev. 5 PDF): "Manage and retain information within the system and information output from the system in accordance with applicable laws, executive orders, directives, regulations, policies, standards, guidelines and operational requirements"; the discussion says retention requirements cover the full information life cycle. Used in crisc_d4_008 (call recordings kept past the approved retention period because deletion was never set up).

### NIST CSWP 29, The NIST Cybersecurity Framework (CSF) 2.0 (Feb 2024)
- NIST CSF 2.0 Section 3.1 (CSF Profiles: a Current Profile describes outcomes being achieved and a Target Profile the outcomes selected for risk management objectives)
- NIST CSF 2.0 GV.RM-03 (cybersecurity risk management activities and outcomes are included in enterprise risk management processes)
- NIST CSF 2.0 GV.RM-07 (strategic opportunities or positive risks are characterized and are included in organizational cybersecurity risk discussions)
- NIST CSF 2.0 GV.RR-01 (organizational leadership is responsible and accountable for cybersecurity risk and fosters a culture that is risk-aware, ethical and continually improving)
- NIST CSF 2.0 GV.RR-02 (roles, responsibilities and authorities related to cybersecurity risk management are established, communicated, understood and enforced)
- NIST CSF 2.0 ID.AM-02 (inventories of software, services and systems managed by the organization are maintained)
- NIST CSF 2.0 ID.IM-03 (improvements are identified from execution of operational processes, procedures and activities)
- NIST CSF 2.0 ID.RA-07 (changes and exceptions are managed, assessed for risk impact, recorded and tracked)
- NIST CSF 2.0 ID.RA-10 (critical suppliers are assessed prior to acquisition)
- NIST CSF 2.0 PR.AT-01 (personnel are provided with awareness and training so that they possess the knowledge and skills to perform general tasks with cybersecurity risks in mind)
- NIST CSF 2.0 PR.PS-02 (software is maintained, replaced and removed commensurate with risk)
- NIST CSF 2.0 RC.RP-03 (the integrity of backups and other restoration assets is verified before using them for restoration)
- NIST CSF 2.0 RS.AN-03 (analysis is performed to establish what has taken place during an incident and the root cause of the incident)
- NIST CSF 2.0 RS.MI-01 (incidents are contained)
  - All Verified, 30 Oct 2026, against the CSWP 29 PDF (nvlpubs.nist.gov/nistpubs/CSWP/NIST.CSWP.29.pdf): each subcategory read in Appendix A. GV.RR-01, GV.RR-02, ID.RA-07 and RS.MI-01 are re-listed from the CC library in the same form. GV.RM-07 reads "Strategic opportunities (i.e., positive risks) are characterized and are included in organizational cybersecurity risk discussions" (used for the opportunity side of emerging technology, crisc_d4_013). Uses: ID.AM-02 and ID.RA-07 in crisc_d4_001; PR.PS-02 in 002; RS.AN-03 and ID.IM-03 in 004; GV.RM-03 in 009 and 010; GV.RR-02 in 010; RS.MI-01 and RC.RP-03 in 012; GV.RM-07 and ID.RA-10 in 013; Section 3.1 and ID.RA-05 in 016; PR.AT-01 and GV.RR-01 in 017; PR.AT-01 in 018. Cross-domain fix 30 Oct 2026: crisc_d4_016 was re-angled to framework alignment and now cites Section 3.1 (Verified 31 Oct 2026 against the CSWP 29 PDF: a Current Profile "specifies the Core outcomes that an organization is currently achieving" and a Target Profile "the desired outcomes that an organization has selected and prioritized for achieving its cybersecurity risk management objectives") with ID.RA-05 from the D2 block; the D4 GV.SC-07 bullet was removed so the library carries one GV.SC-07 string (the D3 one). crisc_d4_017 now uses PR.AT-01 and GV.RR-01 (role-based onboarding training).

### NIST AI 100-1, AI Risk Management Framework (AI RMF 1.0, Jan 2023)
- NIST AI 100-1 (AI RMF 1.0) MANAGE 1.1 (a determination is made as to whether the AI system achieves its intended purposes and whether its development or deployment should proceed)
- NIST AI 100-1 (AI RMF 1.0) MANAGE 3.1 (AI risks and benefits from third-party resources are regularly monitored and risk controls are applied and documented)
- NIST AI 100-1 (AI RMF 1.0) MEASURE 2.11 (fairness and bias as identified in the MAP function are evaluated and results are documented)
- NIST AI 100-1 (AI RMF 1.0) MEASURE 2.3 (AI system performance or assurance criteria are measured and demonstrated for conditions similar to deployment settings)
  - All Verified, 30 Oct 2026, against the AI 100-1 PDF (nvlpubs.nist.gov/nistpubs/ai/NIST.AI.100-1.pdf), Tables 3 and 4. MANAGE 1.1 in full: "A determination is made as to whether the AI system achieves its intended purposes and stated objectives and whether its development or deployment should proceed." Section 1.1 also says AI impacts "can be positive, negative, or both and can result in opportunities or threats" (used in notes 4A7). MEASURE 2.3 in full: "AI system performance or assurance criteria are measured qualitatively or quantitatively and demonstrated for conditions similar to deployment setting(s). Measures are documented." (Table 3; Verified 30 Oct 2026). Uses: MANAGE 3.1 in crisc_d4_006 (vendor model swap); MEASURE 2.11 and MEASURE 2.3 in crisc_d4_014 (housing-assistance ranking tool tested on past council applications). MANAGE 1.1 was used by crisc_d4_014 until the 30 Oct 2026 review fixes and is kept for later batches.

### Other NIST publications
- NIST SP 800-12 Rev. 1 Section 3.4 (Information Owner/Steward)
  - Verified, 30 Oct 2026 (re-listed from CC): PDF read; the owner is the official with authority for specified information who establishes "the rules for the appropriate use and protection of the subject information". Used in crisc_d4_007.
- NIST SP 800-34 Rev. 1 Section 3.2.1 (Determine Business Processes and Recovery Criticality: MTD, RTO and RPO)
  - Verified, 30 Oct 2026 (re-listed from CC): PDF read; still the current revision on csrc.nist.gov. Section 3.4.3 (not cited here) describes cold, warm, hot and mirrored sites, used in notes 4A6.1. Used in crisc_d4_011. MTD is NIST's term; the ISACA Glossary calls it maximum tolerable outage (MTO), and the D4 notes use MTO with MTD named only as "other sources say" (31 Oct 2026).
- NIST SP 800-50 Rev. 1 Section 5.2.3 (Evaluating CPLP Effectiveness)
  - Verified, 30 Oct 2026: Sep 2024 PDF read; Section 5.2.3 says program managers periodically evaluate the learning program's effectiveness with documented metrics and measurements (see its Sec. 2.4) and report results to leadership. Used in crisc_d4_018 (behavior measure over completion). The CC library cites this publication by title only; this bullet adds the section. Section 5.2.3.3 Table 3 (Kirkpatrick Model) separates Level 2 Learning (whether participants absorbed the knowledge) from Level 3 Behavior (whether they apply it at work), which is the line between quiz scores and the upload trend in crisc_d4_018 (Verified 30 Oct 2026).
- NIST SP 800-88 Rev. 2 Section 3.1 (Sanitization Methods: clear, purge and destroy)
  - Verified, 30 Oct 2026 (re-listed from CC): Sep 2025 PDF read. 3.1.2 says purge makes recovery infeasible with state-of-the-art laboratory techniques while keeping the media potentially reusable, and lists cryptographic erase among purge techniques; the section also warns that overwrite habits from magnetic media expose data on flash media. Used in notes 4A4.2; no item cites it since the 31 Oct 2026 re-audit.
- NIST SP 800-128 Section 3.3.2 (Implement the Configuration Change Control Process: unscheduled changes are reviewed by the CCB as soon as practical)
  - Verified, 30 Oct 2026: PDF (Aug 2011, includes updates as of 10 Oct 2019; csrc.nist.gov confirms this is the current final) read. Under "Unscheduled or Unauthorized Changes": unscheduled (emergency) changes "are still managed and controlled" and "are reviewed/resolved by the CCB as soon as is practical after unscheduled changes are made". CCB is the configuration control board. Used in crisc_d4_003.
- NIST Privacy Framework Version 1.0 Section 1.2.1 (Cybersecurity and Privacy Risk Management: privacy risk can arise from data processing unrelated to cybersecurity incidents)
  - Verified, 30 Oct 2026: PDF (NIST.CSWP.01162020, Jan 2020) read. Section 1.2.1 says managing cybersecurity risk "is not sufficient, as privacy risks can also arise by means unrelated to cybersecurity incidents" (Figure 2). The NIST Privacy Framework page lists Version 1.1 only as an initial public draft on 30 Oct 2026, so 1.0 is the citable final. Used in crisc_d4_019.

CSRC pages confirm SP 800-188 (Sep 2023), SP 800-207 (Aug 2020), SP 800-218 (Feb 2022) and SP 800-50 Rev. 1 (Sep 2024) as current finals with no withdrawal.
- ISACA Glossary (enterprise architecture)
  - Verified, 31 Oct 2026: "A description of the fundamental underlying design of the business system components ... the relationships among them and the manner in which they support the enterprise's objectives." Used in crisc_d4_021.

  - Cross-domain fixes 4 Nov 2026: crisc_d4_048 was re-angled to limits on what an autonomous AI maintenance agent may do without approval and now cites NIST AI 100-1 (AI RMF 1.0) GOVERN 3.2 (Domain 3 block) and Appendix C (Domain 3 round 3 block), both re-opened 4 Nov 2026 against https://nvlpubs.nist.gov/nistpubs/ai/NIST.AI.100-1.pdf (re-downloaded) and Verified: GOVERN 3.2 "Policies and procedures are in place to define and differentiate roles and responsibilities for human-AI configurations and oversight of AI systems"; Appendix C item 1, human-AI configurations "can span from fully autonomous to fully manual", some systems may not require human oversight and "Other systems may specifically require human oversight". It no longer cites ISACA Glossary (opportunity) or NIST CSF 2.0 GV.RM-07 (GV.RM-07 stays with crisc_d4_013). crisc_d4_047 was re-angled to minimization at the sharing stage and now cites NIST SP 800-53 Rev. 5 SA-8(33) (Round 2 block above, re-opened and Verified the same day) with CA-3, no longer ISACA Glossary (data owner). No new bullet added; each string already sits in this library.
- NIST SP 800-218 Version 1.1 RV.3 (Analyze Vulnerabilities to Identify Their Root Causes: analyze root causes over time and update the SDLC process so they do not recur)
  - Verified, 4 Nov 2026: RV.3 "Help reduce the frequency of vulnerabilities in the future"; RV.3.2 "Analyze the root causes over time to identify patterns, such as a particular secure coding practice not being followed consistently"; RV.3.4 "Review the SDLC process, and update it if appropriate to prevent (or reduce the likelihood of) the root cause recurring". Used in crisc_d4_052.
- NIST SP 800-53 Rev. 5 CA-3 (Information Exchange)
  - Verified, 4 Nov 2026: approve and manage information exchange using agreements (information exchange security agreements, memoranda of understanding, user agreements, nondisclosure agreements and others) that document security and privacy requirements, controls and responsibilities; the discussion names exchanges of files by email. Used in crisc_d4_047 (since the cross-domain gate, 4 Nov 2026, with SA-8(33): the agreement is in place and the item turns on what each extract carries).
- NIST SP 800-53 Rev. 5 PL-8(1) (Security and Privacy Architectures | Defense in Depth)
  - Verified, 4 Nov 2026: design the architecture "using a defense-in-depth approach"; the discussion says adversaries "must overcome multiple controls to achieve their objective", which raises their work factor and "the likelihood of detection". Used in crisc_d4_053.
- NIST SP 800-53 Rev. 5 PM-5(1) (System Inventory | Inventory of Personally Identifiable Information)
  - Verified, 4 Nov 2026: establish, maintain and update an inventory of all systems, applications and projects that process PII; the discussion says it supports mapping data actions and limiting processing to authorized purposes. Used in crisc_d4_060.
- NIST SP 800-63B-4 Section 3.2.3 (Use of Biometrics: biometric characteristics are not secrets, ways to revoke them are limited, and central storage raises privacy concerns)
  - Verified, 4 Nov 2026 (July 2025 PDF): template protection schemes offer a way to revoke biometric characteristics but "the availability of such solutions is limited"; biometric characteristics "do not constitute secrets"; their use, "especially when stored for central verification, introduces new privacy concerns"; biometric data "SHALL be treated and secured as sensitive personal information". Used in crisc_d4_063.
- NIST CSF 2.0 PR.AT-02 (individuals in specialized roles are provided with awareness and training so that they possess the knowledge and skills to perform relevant tasks with cybersecurity risks in mind)
  - Verified, 4 Nov 2026, against the CSWP 29 PDF (nvlpubs.nist.gov/nistpubs/CSWP/NIST.CSWP.29.pdf, re-downloaded): Appendix A, Awareness and Training (PR.AT), read beside PR.AT-01.
