Each source below was opened on 4 Nov 2026: NIST.SP.800-53r5.pdf, NIST.SP.800-37r2.pdf and nistspecialpublication800-122.pdf were downloaded from nvlpubs.nist.gov with `curl -sS -L`, converted with pdftotext and the control, task or section read; the csrc.nist.gov pages for SP 800-37 Rev. 2 (Dec 2018, supersedes Rev. 1) and SP 800-122 (Apr 2010) show both as current finals with no withdrawal.
- NIST SP 800-53 Rev. 5 AU-2 (Event Logging)
  - Verified, 4 Nov 2026: identify the event types the system can log, "Coordinate the event logging function with other organizational entities requiring audit-related information", and give a rationale for why the selected event types are adequate "to support after-the-fact investigations of incidents". Used in crisc_d4_066 (30 product teams each designing their own logging, with PM-7).
- NIST SP 800-53 Rev. 5 CM-5 (Access Restrictions for Change)
  - Verified, 4 Nov 2026: "Define, document, approve, and enforce physical and logical access restrictions associated with changes to the system"; the discussion says organizations "permit only qualified and authorized individuals to access systems for purposes of initiating changes", including through workflow automation. Used in crisc_d4_067 (pipeline administrators who can switch off the peer-review gate).
- NIST SP 800-37 Rev. 2 Task M-7 (System Disposal: implement a disposal strategy and execute required actions when a system is removed from operation)
  - Verified, 4 Nov 2026: Task M-7 reads "Implement a system disposal strategy and execute required actions when a system is removed from operation"; SDLC phase "Existing – Disposal"; the discussion lists media sanitization, configuration management and record retention, and says inventory systems are updated "to indicate the system that is being removed from service". Used in crisc_d4_068 (a replaced system's database left running).
- NIST SP 800-122 Section 3.2.3 (Data Field Sensitivity: combinations of data fields can be more sensitive than each field alone)
  - Verified, 4 Nov 2026: "Organizations should evaluate the sensitivity of each individual PII data field, as well as the sensitivity of the PII data fields together"; organizations "may also consider certain combinations of PII data fields to be more sensitive ... than each data field would be considered without the existence of the others". Used in crisc_d4_069 (online order records joined with store Wi-Fi logs; re-set from loyalty records at the review fixes, 4 Nov 2026).
- NIST SP 800-53 Rev. 5 SC-29 (Heterogeneity)
- NIST SP 800-53 Rev. 5 PL-8(2) (Security and Privacy Architectures | Supplier Diversity)
  - Both Verified, 4 Nov 2026. SC-29: "Employ a diverse set of information technologies" for organization-defined components; the discussion says diversity "protects against common mode failures" and reduces the likelihood that the means used to compromise one component work against others. PL-8(2): require that controls allocated to locations and architectural layers "are obtained from different suppliers", because products have different strengths and weaknesses. Used in crisc_d4_073 (two same-vendor firewalls in series with a copied rule set). Re-read 4 Nov 2026 for the review fixes (NIST.SP.800-53r5.pdf re-downloaded): the SC-29 discussion also says "An increase in diversity may add complexity and management overhead that could ultimately lead to mistakes and unauthorized configurations", the trade-off crisc_d4_073's explanation now carries.
- NIST SP 800-53 Rev. 5 AT-2 (Literacy Training and Awareness)
  - Verified, 4 Nov 2026: "Provide security and privacy literacy training to system users (including managers, senior executives, and contractors)"; the discussion says content is based on "specific organizational requirements, the systems to which personnel have authorized access, and work environments" and covers how to respond to suspected incidents. Used in crisc_d4_074 (staffing-firm contractors outside an employee-only awareness program).

### Round 8 additions (crisc_d4_086 to 090, 5 Nov 2026)
Each source below was opened on 5 Nov 2026: NIST.CSWP.01162020.pdf (Privacy Framework Version 1.0), NIST.SP.800-50r1.pdf, NIST.SP.800-37r2.pdf and NIST.SP.800-53r5.pdf were downloaded from nvlpubs.nist.gov with `curl -sS -L`, converted with pdftotext and the section, task or control read. The nist.gov Privacy Framework page lists Version 1.0 as the framework and Version 1.1 only as an initial public draft (IPD), so items cite 1.0.
- NIST SP 800-37 Rev. 2 Task R-4 (Authorization Decision: determine whether the risk from operating the system is acceptable, a decision that rests with the authorizing official)
  - Verified, 5 Nov 2026: Task R-4 reads "Determine if the risk from the operation or use of the information system or the provision or use of common controls is acceptable", SDLC phase "New – Implementation/Assessment"; the discussion says "The explicit acceptance of risk is the responsibility of the authorizing official and cannot be delegated" and that the authorization package gives "the most current information" on the system's posture. Fit note: NIST's authorizing official is a senior official; KaiSip maps the role to the accountable business owner of the risk. Used in crisc_d4_086 (a booking system going live without single sign-on, already assessed within appetite with nothing changed).
- NIST Privacy Framework Version 1.0 Section 1.2.1 Figure 3 (Relationship Between Privacy Risk and Organizational Risk: individuals experience the direct impact of problems from data processing, and organizational impacts such as noncompliance costs, customer abandonment and reputational harm follow)
  - Verified, 5 Nov 2026: Section 1.2.1 says individuals "experience the direct impact of problems" and that, as a result, "an organization may experience impacts such as noncompliance costs, revenue loss arising from customer abandonment of products and services, or harm to its external brand reputation or internal culture"; Figure 3 is titled "Relationship Between Privacy Risk and Organizational Risk". Appendix B defines privacy risk as "The likelihood that individuals will experience problems resulting from data processing, and the impact should they occur", and Section 1.2.2 says privacy risk assessments help organizations "distinguish between privacy risk and compliance risk". Used in crisc_d4_089 (a privacy impact assessment that rates risks only by fines and press coverage).
- NIST Privacy Framework Version 1.0 ID.RA-P (Risk Assessment: the organization understands the privacy risks to individuals and how they may create follow-on impacts on organizational operations)
  - Verified, 5 Nov 2026, Appendix A (Privacy Framework Core): the Risk Assessment category (ID.RA-P) reads "The organization understands the privacy risks to individuals and how such privacy risks may create follow-on impacts on organizational operations, including mission, functions, other risk management priorities (e.g., compliance, financial), reputation, workforce, and culture." Used in crisc_d4_089.
- NIST SP 800-50 Rev. 1 Section 2.8 (Establishing the CPLP Plan Priorities: evaluate the organization's critical risk factors to determine learning priorities)
- NIST SP 800-50 Rev. 1 Section 2.1 (Building the Strategic Plan: use risk assessment results to inform the alignment between program development, learning materials and risk management)
  - Both Verified, 5 Nov 2026 (Sep 2024 PDF): Section 2.8 opens "During the planning phase of the CPLP life cycle, evaluate the organization's critical risk factors to determine the learning priorities"; Section 2.1 lists "Use risk assessment results and existing strategies to inform the alignment between program development, learning materials, and risk management". Section 3.1.2.1 also has CPLP managers review "relevant cybersecurity or privacy risks for the participant audience" when identifying learning needs. CPLP is the publication's cybersecurity and privacy learning program. Used in crisc_d4_090 (the basis for a toy maker's awareness topics).
  - Note: the other round 8 parts reuse bullets already in this library: ISACA Glossary (risk acceptance) and (recovery time objective); NIST SP 800-53 Rev. 5 AC-5, AC-6 and RA-8 (AC-6 re-read 5 Nov 2026 in NIST.SP.800-53r5.pdf: least privilege allows "only authorized accesses for users (or processes acting on behalf of users)", which covers an AI assistant's account; used in crisc_d4_087); NIST CSF 2.0 RS.MI-01, RC.RP-03 and PR.AT-01.

### Round 9 additions (5 Nov 2026)
Each source below was opened on 5 Nov 2026: https://www.isaca.org/resources/glossary was downloaded with `curl -sS -L` and each entry read; NIST.SP.800-82r3.pdf was downloaded from nvlpubs.nist.gov and NIST.SP.800-37r2.pdf re-read, both converted with pdftotext and the section or task read. The csrc.nist.gov page for SP 800-82 Rev. 3 (https://csrc.nist.gov/pubs/sp/800/82/r3/final) lists it final, published September 2023, superseding Rev. 2 of 3 Jun 2015.
- ISACA Glossary (workaround)
  - Verified, 5 Nov 2026: "A sequence of actions the user should take to avoid a problem or system limitation until the computer program is changed. They may include manual procedures used in conjunction with the computer system." Used in crisc_d4_092 (an owner-approved workaround held until a March fix).
- ISACA Glossary (data classification)
  - Verified, 5 Nov 2026: "The assignment of a level of sensitivity to data (or information) that results in the specification of controls for each level of classification. Levels of sensitivity of data are assigned according to predefined categories as data are created, amended, enhanced, stored or transmitted." Used in crisc_d4_093 (research data gathered for three months without a class).
- NIST SP 800-37 Rev. 2 Task C-2 (Security Categorization: the system owner and the information owner or steward categorize a new system at initiation, and the results influence control selection)
  - Verified, 5 Nov 2026: Task C-2 "Categorize the system and document the security categorization results"; primary responsibility "System Owner; Information Owner or Steward"; System Development Life Cycle Phase "New – Initiation (concept/requirements definition)"; the discussion says "The results of the categorization process influence the selection of security controls for the system", and a footnote calls the Categorize step "a precondition for the selection of security controls". Used in crisc_d4_093.
- NIST SP 800-82 Rev. 3 Section 3.2.1 (Benefits of Cybersecurity Investments: cyber attacks on OT can have physical impacts, particularly personal injury and loss of life, and damage to the environment)
- NIST SP 800-82 Rev. 3 Section 6.2.10 (Remote Access: provide remote access to OT only if justified and limited to the business need, and never so that it circumvents safety or security controls)
  - Both Verified, 5 Nov 2026 (Guide to Operational Technology (OT) Security, September 2023). Section 3.2.1 lists, among the impacts of cyber attacks, "Physical impacts. Physical impacts encompass the set of direct consequences of OT failure, particularly personal injury and the loss of life. Other effects include the loss of property (including data) and potential damage to the environment." Section 6.2.10's OT-specific guidance says "Remote access should be provided only if justified and limited to what is required for the business need. Remote access should not circumvent or negate safety or security controls." Section 5.1.3 adds that OT systems "are becoming more integrated into business networks and cloud infrastructures" (read for the notes, not cited). Used in crisc_d4_095 (a dairy's digital twin able to change pasteurizer heating settings).
  - Note: the other round 9 parts reuse bullets already in this library: ISACA Glossary (risk acceptance), (problem) and (data owner); NIST IR 8286B-upd1 Section 2.4.3; NIST CSF 2.0 GV.RR-02 and ID.RA-06; NIST SP 800-37 Rev. 2 Task R-4; NIST SP 800-218 Version 1.1 PO.4.1. The risk acceptance, problem and data owner entries were re-read on the 5 Nov 2026 glossary download and are unchanged.

### Round 10 additions (5 Nov 2026)
Each source below was opened on 5 Nov 2026: NIST.SP.800-218.pdf, NIST.SP.800-34r1.pdf (nistspecialpublication800-34r1.pdf), NIST.SP.800-53r5.pdf and NIST.SP.800-207.pdf were downloaded from nvlpubs.nist.gov with `curl -sS -L`, converted with pdftotext and the task, section or control read. The csrc.nist.gov pages show SP 800-218 final February 2022 (SP 800-218 Rev. 1 is still only an initial public draft of 17 Dec 2025), SP 800-34 Rev. 1 May 2010 (updated 11 Nov 2010) and SP 800-207 final August 2020.
- NIST SP 800-218 Version 1.1 PW.1.1 (use forms of risk modeling, such as threat modeling, attack modeling or attack surface mapping, to help assess the security risk for the software)
  - Verified, 5 Nov 2026: PW.1.1 reads "Use forms of risk modeling – such as threat modeling, attack modeling, or attack surface mapping – to help assess the security risk for the software"; its practice, PW.1, is to "Identify and evaluate the security requirements for the software; determine what security risks the software is likely to face during operation and how the software's design and architecture should mitigate those risks". Used in crisc_d4_096 (an online multiplayer game using requirements copied from an offline game).
- NIST SP 800-34 Rev. 1 Section 5.1.2 (Maintenance of Data Security, Integrity, and Backup: key management keeps encrypted backups available, with keys stored separate from, but accessible to, the backup data)
  - Verified, 5 Nov 2026: Section 5.1.2 says "A solid key management process must be established so encrypted data is available as needed" and "These keys should be stored separate from, but accessible to, the primary encrypted backup data." Section 5.2.2 adds that if encrypted data is sent offsite, key management should make sure "the data is readable if it needs to be recovered onto a new or replaced system". Used in crisc_d4_097 (offsite backups whose only keys sit in a vault at the main data center).
- NIST SP 800-53 Rev. 5 SC-12(1) (Cryptographic Key Establishment and Management | Availability)
  - Verified, 5 Nov 2026: the enhancement reads "Maintain availability of information in the event of the loss of cryptographic keys by users"; its discussion names key escrow as a common practice. CP-9(8) (System Backup | Cryptographic Protection) was read beside it: organizations that encrypt backups "also consider cryptographic key management solutions". Used in crisc_d4_097.
- NIST SP 800-207 Section 2.1 (Tenets of Zero Trust: network location alone does not imply trust, and access to each enterprise resource is granted per session)
- NIST SP 800-207 Section 5.3 (Stolen Credentials/Insider Threat: Zero Trust stops compromised accounts or assets from moving laterally, but a valid account can still reach the resources it is granted)
  - Both Verified, 5 Nov 2026. Section 2.1, tenet 2: "All communication is secured regardless of network location. Network location alone does not imply trust"; tenet 3: "Access to individual enterprise resources is granted on a per-session basis." Section 5.3: "ZTA reduces risk and prevents any compromised accounts or assets from moving laterally throughout the network", while "an attacker with valid credentials (or a malicious insider) may still be able to access resources for which the account has been granted access". Used in crisc_d4_098 (which register scenario a working Zero Trust control changes most). This moves Section 2.1 from "Opened for the notes only" to a keyed bullet.
- NIST SP 800-53 Rev. 5 PT-4(1) (Consent | Tailored Consent)
  - Verified, 5 Nov 2026: PT-4(1) reads "Provide [Assignment: organization-defined mechanisms] to allow individuals to tailor processing permissions to selected elements of personally identifiable information"; its discussion says "While some processing may be necessary for the basic functionality of the product or service, other processing may not. In these circumstances, organizations allow individuals to select how specific personally identifiable information elements may be processed." The base control PT-4 (Consent) names opt-in and opt-out as types of consent. Used in crisc_d4_100 (agreement to AI training made a condition of ordering).
  - Note: the other round 10 parts reuse bullets already in this library: NIST SP 800-37 Rev. 2 Task P-14 (crisc_d4_096); NIST CSF 2.0 PR.AT-01 and NIST SP 800-53 Rev. 5 AT-2 (crisc_d4_099; AT-2 re-read 5 Nov 2026 in NIST.SP.800-53r5.pdf: literacy training and awareness for system users "including managers, senior executives, and contractors"); NIST SP 800-53 Rev. 5 PT-3 (crisc_d4_100).

### Round 11 additions (5 Nov 2026)
Each source below was opened on 5 Nov 2026: NIST.SP.800-128.pdf (https://nvlpubs.nist.gov/nistpubs/SpecialPublications/NIST.SP.800-128.pdf) and NIST.IR.8202.pdf (https://nvlpubs.nist.gov/nistpubs/ir/2018/NIST.IR.8202.pdf) were downloaded with `curl -sS -L`, converted with pdftotext and the section read; the csrc.nist.gov pages (https://csrc.nist.gov/pubs/sp/800/128/upd1/final and https://csrc.nist.gov/pubs/ir/8202/final) show SP 800-128 as "August 2011 (includes updates as of 10-10-2019)" and IR 8202 as "October 2018", with no withdrawal or newer revision listed.
- NIST SP 800-128 Section 3.1.2 (Planning at the System Level: the system component inventory can record each component's relationships to and dependencies on other systems, and adds value when it supports security impact analysis of changes)
  - Verified, 5 Nov 2026: Section 3.1.2, under the system component inventory, says "A system component inventory adds real value to SecCM when each item in the inventory is associated with information that can be leveraged for determination of approved configuration baselines, configuration change control/security impact analysis, and monitoring/reporting"; its list of additional data elements includes "Relationships to other system components in the inventory", "Relationships to/dependencies on other systems" and "Other systems supported by this component", and footnote 19 says the inventory's record of a server's uses "can assist in the quick identification of the applications that are impacted". The CI data elements in the same section include "Relationship to/dependencies on other CIs within the system". Used in crisc_d4_102 (a sign-in server change, tested only with the system its impact assessment named, broke badge readers in 40 buildings).
- NIST IR 8202 Section 8.1 (Additional Blockchain Considerations: data on a permissionless blockchain is generally public, personal data may not be appropriate to store, and records can be amended by new transactions but not removed)
  - Verified, 5 Nov 2026 (Blockchain Technology Overview, October 2018). Section 8.1, Data Visibility: "Permissionless blockchain networks can allow anyone to inspect and contribute to the blockchain. The data is generally public", and for data governed by "Personally Identifiable Information (PII) or General Data Protection Regulation (GDPR) regulations", it "may or may not be appropriate to store even within a permissioned blockchain network". Under "Tamper evident and tamper resistant data": "With a blockchain, there is only 'CR' (create, read)"; older data can be deprecated, "but there is no removal process for the original data", and new transactions can amend and update previous ones. Section 7.1 adds that ledgers are "tamper evident and tamper resistant" though not strictly immutable (a 51% attack is possible but costly). Used in crisc_d4_105 (recipients' names and locker locations written to a public blockchain).
  - Note: the other round 11 parts reuse bullets already in this library: NIST SP 800-53 Rev. 5 SA-22 (crisc_d4_101; re-read 5 Nov 2026 in NIST.SP.800-53r5.pdf: "The increased risk of using unsupported system components can be mitigated, for example, by prohibiting the connection of such components to public or uncontrolled networks, or implementing other forms of isolation"); NIST CSF 2.0 PR.PS-02 (crisc_d4_101); NIST SP 800-53 Rev. 5 CM-4 (crisc_d4_102; re-read 5 Nov 2026: impact analyses review "system design documentation and operational procedures to understand control implementation and how specific system changes might affect the controls"); NIST SP 800-53 Rev. 5 SI-12, ISACA Glossary (data owner) and (data custodian) (crisc_d4_103); COBIT 2019 APO05 and ISACA Now Blog 23 September 2019 (crisc_d4_104).

### Round 12 additions (5 Nov 2026)
Each source below was opened on 5 Nov 2026: NIST.SP.800-218.pdf (https://nvlpubs.nist.gov/nistpubs/SpecialPublications/NIST.SP.800-218.pdf, SSDF Version 1.1, February 2022), NIST.FIPS.199.pdf (https://nvlpubs.nist.gov/nistpubs/FIPS/NIST.FIPS.199.pdf, February 2004), NIST.CSWP.29.pdf (CSF 2.0), NIST.SP.800-50r1.pdf (September 2024) and NIST.CSWP.01162020.pdf (Privacy Framework Version 1.0) were downloaded from nvlpubs.nist.gov with `curl -sS -L`, converted with pdftotext and the task, section or subcategory read.
- NIST SP 800-218 Version 1.1 RV.1.3 (have a policy that addresses vulnerability disclosure and remediation, and implement the roles, responsibilities and processes needed to support it)
  - Verified, 5 Nov 2026: RV.1.3 reads "Have a policy that addresses vulnerability disclosure and remediation, and implement the roles, responsibilities, and processes needed to support that policy"; Example 1 is "Establish a vulnerability disclosure program, and make it easy for security researchers to learn about your program and report possible vulnerabilities". Used in crisc_d4_106 (a public route for outside flaw reports, with set reply times).
- NIST SP 800-218 Version 1.1 RV.1.1 (gather information from software acquirers, users and public sources on potential vulnerabilities and investigate all credible reports)
  - Verified, 5 Nov 2026: RV.1.1 reads "Gather information from software acquirers, users, and public sources on potential vulnerabilities in the software and third-party components that the software uses, and investigate all credible reports". Used in crisc_d4_106.
- NIST FIPS 199 Section 3 (Categorization of Information and Information Systems: impact is rated for confidentiality, integrity and availability, and integrity includes non-repudiation and authenticity)
  - Verified, 5 Nov 2026: Section 3 bases security categories on potential impact and, under Security Objectives, defines integrity as "Guarding against improper information modification or destruction, and includes ensuring information non-repudiation and authenticity", with "A loss of integrity is the unauthorized modification or destruction of information"; impact levels are set for each of the three objectives. Used in crisc_d4_108 (an encrypted archive of signed contracts rated only on exposure).
- NIST CSF 2.0 PR.DS-01 (the confidentiality, integrity and availability of data-at-rest are protected)
  - Verified, 5 Nov 2026: PR.DS-01 reads "The confidentiality, integrity, and availability of data-at-rest are protected". Used in crisc_d4_108.
- NIST SP 800-50 Rev. 1 Section 2.4.1.1 (Quantitative Measurements: metrics on incident reporting that show employees can recognize and report potential events, and the ability to recognize and report privacy disclosures)
  - Verified, 5 Nov 2026: Section 2.4.1.1 lists, among quantitative learning program data, "Metrics on incident reporting that demonstrate employee ability to recognize and report potential cybersecurity events", "Longitudinal data that depicts program impacts, including employee behavior change, over time" and "Ability to recognize and report privacy information disclosures or misuse, as evidenced by employee adoption of reporting tools"; it also lists closed-ended survey feedback and completion rates as separate measures. Used in crisc_d4_109 (the share of filter-caught misdirected payroll files that senders reported).
- NIST Privacy Framework Version 1.0 CT.DP-P1 (data are processed to limit observability and linkability, for example with data actions taking place on local devices)
  - Verified, 5 Nov 2026: under Disassociated Processing (CT.DP-P), "Data processing solutions increase disassociability consistent with the organization's risk strategy to protect individuals' privacy and enable implementation of privacy principles (e.g., data minimization)"; CT.DP-P1 reads "Data are processed to limit observability and linkability (e.g., data actions take place on local devices, privacy-preserving cryptography)". Used in crisc_d4_110 (delivery drones that process video on board and keep none).
  - Note: the other round 12 parts reuse bullets already in this library: NIST CSF 2.0 PR.DS-11 and NIST SP 800-34 Rev. 1 Section 3.2.1 (crisc_d4_107, a trained navigation model kept only at the main site; PR.DS-11 re-read 5 Nov 2026 in NIST.CSWP.29.pdf: "Backups of data are created, protected, maintained, and tested"), and NIST SP 800-53 Rev. 5 SA-8(33) (crisc_d4_110).

### Round 13 additions (12 Nov 2026)
Each source below was opened on 12 Nov 2026: NIST.SP.800-88r2.pdf (https://nvlpubs.nist.gov/nistpubs/SpecialPublications/NIST.SP.800-88r2.pdf) was downloaded with `curl -sS -L`, converted with pdftotext and the sections read; the csrc.nist.gov page (https://csrc.nist.gov/pubs/sp/800/88/r2/final) shows "Date Published: September 2025" and "Supersedes: SP 800-88 Rev. 1 (12/17/2014)", with no withdrawal.
- NIST SP 800-88 Rev. 2 Section 4.1 (Storage Sanitization Policy: align the data classification scheme with minimum acceptable sanitization methods, and set requirements for disposal and reuse)
  - Verified, 12 Nov 2026: Section 4.1 says a storage sanitization policy should address "Alignment of the organization's data classification scheme (e.g., low, medium, and high security categorizations) with minimum acceptable sanitization methods (i.e., clear, purge, and destroy)" and "Requirements for the disposal and/or reuse of storage assets". Used in crisc_d4_113 (a satellite imagery firm that overwrites every retired drive once, whatever it held and wherever it goes).
- NIST SP 800-88 Rev. 2 Section 4.3 (Storage Sanitization and Disposition Decision Framework: decisions are commensurate with the confidentiality of the information, and purge may suit better than destroy for lease returns, reuse, resale or cost)
  - Verified, 12 Nov 2026: Section 4.3 says purge (and clear, where applicable) "may be more appropriate than destroy sanitization techniques when factoring in contractual obligations (e.g., lease returns), environmental concerns, the desire to reuse the ISM (either within the organization or by selling or donating the ISM), the cost of an ISM", that organizations make sanitization decisions "commensurate with the security categorization (i.e., low, medium, and high) of the confidentiality of information contained on their media", and that "The decision process is based on the confidentiality of the information rather than the type of media"; Section 4.3.4 adds that control changes when media is returned from a lease, donated or resold. Section 2.4 says "The cost versus benefit trade-off of a sanitization process should be understood prior to a final decision". Used in crisc_d4_113 (destroying every drive is the runner-up: it scraps reusable drives where risk is low).
  - Note: the other round 13 parts reuse bullets already in this library. ISACA Glossary (enterprise architecture) and NIST SP 800-53 Rev. 5 PM-7 (crisc_d4_111, a loan-boarding assessment scoped from a repository older than an acquisition; PM-7 re-read 12 Nov 2026 in NIST.SP.800-53r5.pdf: "Develop and maintain an enterprise architecture with consideration for information security, privacy, and the resulting risk"). NIST SP 800-53 Rev. 5 AC-5 and CM-5 with NIST AI 100-1 (AI RMF 1.0) GOVERN 3.2 (Domain 3 block) (crisc_d4_112, an AI coding agent that merges its own fixes; AC-5 re-read: separation of duties "addresses the potential for abuse of authorized privileges" and includes "dividing mission or business functions and support functions among different individuals or roles"). COBIT 2019 APO05 (Domain 1 block) with ISACA Glossary (residual risk) and (risk response) (Domain 2 block) (crisc_d4_114, judging a finished risk-reduction project against the target its business case promised; the risk response entry's "future residual risk" is KaiSip's target risk). NIST SP 800-53 Rev. 5 SA-4 and SA-22 (crisc_d4_115, valve controllers expected to run ten years with three years of patches; SA-4 re-read: security and privacy requirements go "in the acquisition contract"; SA-22's discussion counts "software patches, firmware updates" as support and names isolation as a mitigation once support has ended).

### Round 14 additions (12 Nov 2026)
Each source below was opened on 12 Nov 2026: NIST.SP.800-218.pdf, NIST.SP.800-53r5.pdf, NIST.SP.800-50r1.pdf (all https://nvlpubs.nist.gov/nistpubs/SpecialPublications/) and NIST.AI.600-1.pdf (https://nvlpubs.nist.gov/nistpubs/ai/NIST.AI.600-1.pdf) were downloaded with `curl -sS -L`, converted with pdftotext and the practice, control or section read. Status as recorded above: SP 800-218 Version 1.1 (Feb 2022) is still the final SSDF (Rev. 1 is an initial public draft), SP 800-50 Rev. 1 is the Sep 2024 final, and AI 600-1 is the July 2024 final.
- NIST SP 800-218 Version 1.1 PW.4.1 (acquire and maintain well-secured third-party software components, for example from repositories of sanctioned and vetted open-source components)
  - Verified, 12 Nov 2026: PW.4.1 reads "Acquire and maintain well-secured software components (e.g., software libraries, modules, middleware, frameworks) from commercial, open-source, and other third-party developers for use by the organization's software"; its examples include "Establish one or more software repositories to host sanctioned and vetted open-source components" and obtaining provenance information for each component. PW.4.4 (verify that acquired components comply with the organization's requirements) lists checks for "publicly known vulnerabilities" among its examples. Used in crisc_d4_116 (a package an AI coding assistant named, published by an attacker with malware: vetting at entry is keyed; a known-vulnerability scan is the runner-up, as a new package has no reported flaw).
- NIST AI 600-1 Section 2.2 (Confabulation: generative AI confidently presents erroneous or false content, and risks arise when users believe and act on it)
  - Verified, 12 Nov 2026: Section 2.2 says confabulation "refers to a phenomenon in which GAI systems generate and confidently present erroneous or false content in response to prompts", colloquially "hallucinations", and that risks "may arise when users believe false content ... leading users to act upon or promote the false information". Used in crisc_d4_116 (the assistant named a package that did not exist).
- NIST SP 800-53 Rev. 5 CP-9 (System Backup: backups are conducted at a frequency consistent with recovery time and recovery point objectives)
  - Verified, 12 Nov 2026: CP-9 a. reads "Conduct backups of user-level information contained in [Assignment: organization-defined system components] [Assignment: organization-defined frequency consistent with recovery time and recovery point objectives]"; b. and c. use the same frequency wording. SP 800-34 Rev. 1 Section 3.4.2 adds that policies set "the minimum frequency and scope of backups ... based on data criticality and the frequency that new information is introduced". Used in crisc_d4_117 (nightly backups against a one-hour RPO the registration owner set).
- NIST SP 800-53 Rev. 5 SC-24 (Fail in Known State: failure in a known state addresses security concerns in accordance with the mission and business needs of organizations)
  - Verified, 12 Nov 2026: SC-24 reads "Fail to a [Assignment: organization-defined known system state] for the following failures ..."; its discussion says "Failure in a known state addresses security concerns in accordance with the mission and business needs of organizations" and "prevents the loss of confidentiality, integrity, or availability of information in the event of failures". Used in crisc_d4_118 (bike docks that release bikes when cut off from the server: with both costs analyzed, the risk owner chooses the failure setting).
- NIST SP 800-50 Rev. 1 Section 2.6 (Determining Scope and Complexity: the goal is behavior change, not compliance alone, and material fits the learner's role and learning needs)
  - Verified, 12 Nov 2026: Section 2.6 opens "The ultimate goal of a CPLP is to reduce cybersecurity and privacy risks to the organization through cultural and behavioral changes, not simply to achieve compliance", says material is "commensurate with the role of the person who will undergo the learning effort", and that learners "will appreciate the effort made to ensure that they understand the material in a manner appropriate to their learning needs and the nature of the work that they do". Section 2.1 adds that the strategic plan should "Plan for and support the needs of a diverse workforce". Used in crisc_d4_119 (a yearly English video course at clinics where most staff speak little English).
  - Note: the other round 14 parts reuse bullets already in this library: NIST SP 800-34 Rev. 1 Section 3.2.1 (crisc_d4_117; re-read 12 Nov 2026: the RPO "is a factor of how much data loss the mission/business process can tolerate during the recovery process"), ISACA Glossary (risk owner) (Domain 1 block; crisc_d4_118), NIST SP 800-53 Rev. 5 AT-2 (crisc_d4_119; re-read 12 Nov 2026: content is based on "work environments" among other factors), and NIST Privacy Framework Version 1.0 Section 1.2.1 Figure 3, NIST SP 800-53 Rev. 5 SA-8(33) and ISACA Glossary (data owner) (crisc_d4_120, a scanned 1970s school register with disciplinary notes about pupils likely still alive; SA-8(33) re-read 12 Nov 2026: organizations "should only process personally identifiable information that is directly relevant and necessary to accomplish an authorized purpose", and PT-3's discussion counts dissemination and disclosure as processing).

### Round 15 additions (12 Nov 2026)
Each source below was opened on 12 Nov 2026: NIST.SP.800-53r5.pdf (https://nvlpubs.nist.gov/nistpubs/SpecialPublications/NIST.SP.800-53r5.pdf) and NIST.AI.600-1.pdf (https://nvlpubs.nist.gov/nistpubs/ai/NIST.AI.600-1.pdf) were downloaded with `curl -sS -L`, converted with pdftotext and the control or section read. Status as recorded above: SP 800-53 Rev. 5 (catalog release 5.2.0, Aug 2025) and AI 600-1 (July 2024 final).
- NIST SP 800-53 Rev. 5 SA-8(7) (Security and Privacy Engineering Principles | Reduced Complexity: running older and newer technologies side by side during a transition temporarily increases system complexity)
  - Verified, 12 Nov 2026: SA-8(7) reads "Implement the security design principle of reduced complexity in [Assignment: organization-defined systems or system components]"; its discussion says a small and simple design "is more understandable, more analyzable, and less prone to error", and that "Transitioning from older technologies to newer technologies ... may require implementing the older and newer technologies simultaneously during the transition period. This may result in a temporary increase in system complexity during the transition." RA-5(10)'s discussion adds that during such transitions "some system components may inadvertently be unmanaged and create opportunities for adversary exploitation". Used in crisc_d4_121 (an auto parts supplier's ERP move that runs old and new systems side by side while its architecture review covered only the finished design).
- NIST SP 800-53 Rev. 5 SI-2 (Flaw Remediation: test updates for effectiveness and side effects before installation, install security-relevant updates within an organization-defined time period, and incorporate flaw remediation into configuration management)
  - Verified, 12 Nov 2026: SI-2 reads "a. Identify, report, and correct system flaws; b. Test software and firmware updates related to flaw remediation for effectiveness and potential side effects before installation; c. Install security-relevant software and firmware updates within [Assignment: organization-defined time period] of the release of the updates; and d. Incorporate flaw remediation into the organizational configuration management process." Its discussion says the time periods "may vary based on a variety of risk factors, including ... the criticality of the update (i.e., severity of the vulnerability related to the discovered flaw), the organizational risk tolerance ... or the threat environment", and that organizations "determine the type of testing needed". Used in crisc_d4_122 (a patch for an actively exploited flaw released during a marketplace's peak-season change freeze: the emergency change route is keyed).
- NIST AI 600-1 Section 2.4 (Data Privacy: models may leak sensitive information included in their training data, a problem referred to as data memorization)
  - Verified, 12 Nov 2026: Section 2.4 says "Models may leak, generate, or correctly infer sensitive information about individuals. For example, during adversarial attacks, LLMs have revealed sensitive information (from the public domain) that was included in their training data. This problem has been referred to as data memorization, and may pose exacerbated privacy risks even for data present only in a small number of training samples." Action GV-1.6-003 lists "Special rights and considerations for intellectual property, licensed works, or personal, privileged, proprietary or sensitive data". Used in crisc_d4_123 (a law firm that would fine-tune a drafting assistant on all closed matter files for every lawyer, while most files are open only to their matter team).
  - Note: the other round 15 parts reuse bullets already in this library: NIST CSF 2.0 ID.RA-07 (crisc_d4_121); NIST SP 800-128 Section 3.3.2 (crisc_d4_122); NIST SP 800-53 Rev. 5 AC-6 and ISACA Glossary (data classification) (crisc_d4_123; AC-6 re-read 12 Nov 2026 in NIST.SP.800-53r5.pdf: "allowing only authorized accesses for users (or processes acting on behalf of users) that are necessary to accomplish assigned organizational tasks"); NIST CSF 2.0 GV.SC-07 (Domain 3 block), ISACA Glossary (risk transfer) and (risk owner) (crisc_d4_124, a vendor building a hospital's patient portal for a fixed price, reporting only dates and budget); NIST SP 800-37 Rev. 2 Task P-14 and NIST CSF 2.0 GV.RM-07 (crisc_d4_125, a statistics office reopening phone location data it turned down in 2023 once a telecom offers aggregated counts).

### Round 16 additions (12 Nov 2026)
Each source below was opened on 12 Nov 2026: NIST.SP.800-218.pdf, NIST.SP.800-53r5.pdf and NIST.SP.800-207.pdf (https://nvlpubs.nist.gov/nistpubs/SpecialPublications/) and nistspecialpublication800-34r1.pdf (https://nvlpubs.nist.gov/nistpubs/Legacy/SP/nistspecialpublication800-34r1.pdf) were downloaded with `curl -sS -L`, converted with `pdftotext -layout` and the practice, control or section read. The csrc.nist.gov page for SP 800-34 Rev. 1 shows May 2010 (updated 11/11/2010) with no withdrawal; status for SP 800-218 Version 1.1 (Feb 2022 final; Rev. 1 still an initial public draft), SP 800-53 Rev. 5 (catalog release 5.2.0, Aug 2025) and SP 800-207 (Aug 2020 final) as recorded above.
- NIST SP 800-218 Version 1.1 PW.9 (Configure Software to Have Secure Settings by Default: reduce the likelihood of software being deployed with weak security settings)
  - Verified, 12 Nov 2026: the practice reads "Configure Software to Have Secure Settings by Default (PW.9): Help improve the security of the software at the time of installation to reduce the likelihood of the software being deployed with weak security settings, putting it at greater risk of compromise." PW.9.1 defines "a secure baseline ... so that the default settings are secure and do not weaken the security functions provided by the platform, network infrastructure, or services". Used in crisc_d4_126 (an infusion pump design that gives every unit one administrator password and leaves hospitals to change it).
- NIST SP 800-53 Rev. 5 IA-5 (Authenticator Management: default authenticators are changed before first use, as factory default credentials are often well known and a significant risk)
  - Verified, 12 Nov 2026: IA-5 e. reads "Changing default authenticators prior to first use"; the discussion says "Developers may deliver system components with factory default authentication credentials (i.e., passwords) to allow for initial installation and configuration. Default authentication credentials are often well known, easily discoverable, and present a significant risk." Used in crisc_d4_126.
- NIST SP 800-34 Rev. 1 Section 3.5.4 (TT&E Program Summary: a full-scale functional exercise for a high-impact system includes failover to the alternate location and full recovery and reconstitution to a known state)
  - Verified, 12 Nov 2026: Section 3.5.4 says that for high-impact systems "a full-scale functional exercise ... should include a system failover to the alternate location" and "The test should also include a full recovery and reconstitution of the information system to a known state"; the TT&E activities table that follows describes alternate processing site recovery as including "a full recovery and return to normal operations to a known secure state". Used in crisc_d4_127 (a satellite operator whose switchover tests always stop once the standby ground station has control).
- NIST SP 800-34 Rev. 1 Section 4.4 (Reconstitution Phase: recovery activities are completed, normal operations resume and the system is validated by testing before the plan is deactivated)
  - Verified, 12 Nov 2026: Section 4.4 says "The Reconstitution Phase is the third and final phase of ISCP implementation and defines the actions taken to test and validate system capability and functionality. During Reconstitution, recovery activities are completed and normal system operations are resumed"; it consists of "validating successful recovery and deactivation of the plan". Used in crisc_d4_127.
- NIST SP 800-207 Section 5.7 (Use of Non-person Entities in ZTA Administration: software agents, AI included, are assumed to authenticate when calling resources, and an attacker may induce an agent to perform tasks the attacker is not privileged to perform)
  - Wording corrected at the round 16 standards audit, 12 Nov 2026: the section says how agents authenticate "is an open issue" and that "most automated technology systems will use some means to authenticate when using an API to resource components", so the bullet says "assumed", not "must".
  - Verified, 12 Nov 2026: Section 5.7 says "Artificial intelligence and other software-based agents are being deployed to manage security issues on enterprise networks" and "It is assumed that most automated technology systems will use some means to authenticate when using an API to resource components"; it names the risk that "an attacker will be able to induce or coerce an NPE to perform some task that the attacker is not privileged to perform", and that the agent "may have a lower bar for authentication (e.g., API key versus MFA)". Section 2.1 tenet 4 adds that "client identity can include the user account (or service identity)". Used in crisc_d4_128 (a home insurer's AI claims agent calling a payments service that accepts any request from inside the data center network).
  - Note: the other round 16 parts reuse bullets already in this library: NIST SP 800-207 Section 2.1 (crisc_d4_128; re-read 12 Nov 2026: tenet 2, "Network location alone does not imply trust", and tenet 3, "Access to individual enterprise resources is granted on a per-session basis"); NIST CSF 2.0 PR.AT-02 and ISACA Glossary (risk owner) (crisc_d4_129, a cosmetics brand's newly appointed risk owners handing every accept-or-treat decision back to the risk team; PR.AT-02 re-read 12 Nov 2026 in NIST.CSWP.29.pdf); NIST SP 800-53 Rev. 5 PT-3 and SA-8(33) (crisc_d4_130, ad tracking code on a tax agency's online filing pages; re-read 12 Nov 2026: PT-3 c. restricts processing "to only that which is compatible with the identified purpose(s)", and its discussion counts "dissemination, disclosure" as processing; SA-8(33): "only process personally identifiable information that is directly relevant and necessary to accomplish an authorized purpose").

### Round 22 additions (12 Nov 2026)
Items crisc_d4_156 to crisc_d4_160. Each source below was opened on 12 Nov 2026: NIST.SP.800-53r5.pdf and NIST.SP.800-50r1.pdf (https://nvlpubs.nist.gov/nistpubs/SpecialPublications/), NIST.AI.100-2e2025.pdf and NIST.AI.600-1.pdf (https://nvlpubs.nist.gov/nistpubs/ai/), nistspecialpublication800-34r1.pdf (https://nvlpubs.nist.gov/nistpubs/Legacy/SP/) and NIST.CSWP.29.pdf (https://nvlpubs.nist.gov/nistpubs/CSWP/) were downloaded with `curl -sS -L`, converted with `pdftotext -layout` and the control, section or subcategory read; the ISACA Glossary page (https://www.isaca.org/resources/glossary) was fetched with `curl -sS -L` and the entries read. Wording in the parentheses is our own paraphrase or the official title.
- ISACA Glossary (defense in depth)
  - Verified, 12 Nov 2026: "The practice of layering defenses to provide added protection. Defense in depth improves security by increasing the effort needed in an attack by placing multiple barriers between an attacker and enterprise computing and information resources." Used in crisc_d4_158 (a soap maker's AI assistant whose only safeguard, a prompt filter, testers got past by rewording requests).
- NIST AI 100-2 E2025 Section 3.3.3 (Mitigations: detection systems that classify user input or model output can add assurance through defense in depth, but are themselves open to attack and may fail together with the model they monitor)
  - Verified, 12 Nov 2026, Section 3.3.3 (Mitigations for direct prompting attacks), deployment-time interventions, printed page 48: LLM-based detection systems "classify user input and/or model output as harmful or undesirable. These may provide supplementary assurance through a defense-in-depth philosophy. However, these detection systems are also vulnerable to attacks and may have correlated failures to the main models that they are monitoring." Section 3.4.4, printed pages 53 to 54, adds that "current mitigations do not offer full protection against all attacker techniques", so designers may assume prompt injection is possible when a model is exposed to untrusted input. Used in crisc_d4_158 with NIST SP 800-53 Rev. 5 PL-8(1) (Round 2 block above; re-read 12 Nov 2026: adversaries "must overcome multiple controls to achieve their objective"). Checked, not cited: NIST AI 600-1 Section 2.9 (Information Security) names prompt injection, including indirect injection through data likely to be retrieved, as part of the attack surface generative AI adds.
- NIST SP 800-53 Rev. 5 SR-5(2) (Acquisition Strategies, Tools, and Methods | Assessments Prior to Selection, Acceptance, Modification, or Update)
  - Verified, 12 Nov 2026: SR-5(2) reads "Assess the system, system component, or system service prior to selection, acceptance, modification, or update"; its discussion says organizational personnel or independent entities conduct the assessments, which "can include evaluations; design proposal reviews; visual or physical inspection; ... simulations; ... stress testing", and that the evidence generated is documented for follow-on actions. Used in crisc_d4_157 (a start-up's moisture sensors with lab-only accuracy figures, none run in a dusty gin), with NIST CSF 2.0 ID.RA-10 (Domain 4 block above; re-read 12 Nov 2026 in NIST.CSWP.29.pdf: "Critical suppliers are assessed prior to acquisition").
- NIST SP 800-50 Rev. 1 Section 2.7.1 (Awareness Activities: awareness activities run on an ongoing basis throughout the year, by campaign or in response to threats)
  - Verified, 12 Nov 2026 (September 2024 final), Section 2.7.1, printed page 30: "Cybersecurity and privacy awareness learning activities should be conducted on an ongoing basis throughout the year"; "Activities can be campaign-oriented or ad hoc based on the subject matter, threats, or vulnerabilities or take place during seasonal events". Section 2.7 (printed page 29) adds that employees "may initially believe that the CPLP is the annual or regularly scheduled training". Used in crisc_d4_159 (a dry cleaner chain whose simulation clicks are lowest after its January course and climb every month until the next), with NIST CSF 2.0 PR.AT-01 (Domain 4 block above; re-read 12 Nov 2026 in NIST.CSWP.29.pdf).
- ISACA Glossary (consent)
  - Verified, 12 Nov 2026: "Any freely given, specific, informed and unambiguous indication of the data subject's wishes by which he or she, by a statement or by a clear affirmative action, signifies agreement to the processing of personal data relating to him or her". Used in crisc_d4_160 (a retailer whose policy requires consent for marketing emails plans to email a list bought from a magazine).
- NIST SP 800-53 Rev. 5 PT-4 (Consent)
  - Verified, 12 Nov 2026: PT-4 reads "Implement [Assignment: organization-defined tools or mechanisms] for individuals to consent to the processing of their personally identifiable information prior to its collection that facilitate individuals' informed decision-making"; its discussion names opt-in and opt-out as types of consent and a mechanism to revoke consent. Used in crisc_d4_160. (The enhancement PT-4(1) has its own bullet in the Round 10 block above.)
  - Note: the other round 22 parts reuse bullets already in this library, each re-read on 12 Nov 2026: ISACA Glossary (recovery time objective) ("The amount of time allowed for the recovery of a business function or resource after a disaster occurs") and NIST SP 800-34 Rev. 1 Section 3.5.3 (Domain 2 block; "Functional exercises allow personnel to validate their operational readiness for emergencies by performing their duties in a simulated operational environment") (crisc_d4_156: a failover test marked passed when the servers ran at the backup site, with no user trying them).

  - ISO/IEC 27001:2022: named in notes 4B1.3 as context (crisc_d4_016 no longer names it after the 30 Oct 2026 re-angle); iso.org is blocked, so no ISO bullet is proposed.
  - COBIT 2019 BAI06, DSS02, DSS03, APO03, APO05, BAI11, DSS04, APO14: not cited. The isaca.org COBIT page I opened lists no objective titles; a third-party search result showed "DSS03 Managed Problems", "BAI06 Managed IT Changes" and "APO03 Managed Enterprise Architecture", which would be Likely only. The D1 bullets cite an ISACA white paper Figure 2 that may confirm these titles for a later batch.
